\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}\cap\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\varnothing
, in the proof of Lemma
6.9
we will find it convenient to overload notation and write
\mathfrak{p}_{s}(x)
for the density function
\mathfrak{p}_{s}(x)
when
x\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
and for the mass function
\mathfrak{p}_{s}(x)
when
x\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}
.

To define the transition probability distributions we first define the orthogonality measure for the continuous dual Hahn orthogonal polynomials.

Definition 6.3
.

Assume that
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
with
a+b,a+c>0
or
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
b=\bar{c}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with
\textup{Re}(b)=\textup{Re}(c)>0
. For such
a,b,c
, define

\mathsf{CDH}^{c}(x;a,b,c):=\dfrac{1}{8\pi}\,\cdot\,\dfrac{\bigg\rvert\Gamma\left(a+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{x}}{2},b+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{x}}{2},c+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{x}}{2}\right)\bigg\rvert^{2}}{\Gamma(a+b,a+c,b+c)\cdot\sqrt{x}\cdot\left\rvert\Gamma\left({i}\sqrt{x}\right)\right\rvert^{2}}{\bf 1}_{x>0}.

(77)

Definition 6.4
.

Assume that
a<0
and that
\mathcal{F}
is a finite subset of
\mathbb{R}

with size
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
and elements
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\lfloor-a\rfloor}
. Assume that
b,c
and
j
satisfy one of the three conditions:

•

b=\bar{c}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with

\textup{Re}(b),\textup{Re}(c)>0
, and
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-a\rfloor\rrbracket
;

•

b,c\in\mathbb{R}
with

a+b,a+c>0
, and
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-a\rfloor\rrbracket
;

•

b,c\in\mathbb{R}
and
b,b+c,c-a>0,
with
a+b=-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, and
j\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
.

For
a,b,c
,
\mathcal{F}
, and
j
as above, define

\mathsf{CDH}^{d}(x_{j};a,b,c;\mathcal{F}):=\dfrac{[2a,a+b,a+c]_{j}\cdot(a+j)\cdot\Gamma(b-a,c-a)}{[1,a-b+1,a-c+1]_{j}\cdot a\cdot\Gamma(-2a,b+c)}\,\cdot\,(-1)^{j}.

(78)

For all other first arguments, define
\mathsf{CDH}^{d}(\cdot;a,b,c;\mathcal{F})=0
.

Definition 6.5
.

We will define the measure
\mathsf{CDH}(V;a,b,c;\mathcal{F})
(for Borel subsets
V
of
\mathbb{R}
) under three possible sets of conditions on parameters:

P:

For
a\geq 0
;
\mathcal{F}=\varnothing
; and either
b=\bar{c}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with

\textup{Re}(b),\textup{Re}(c)>0
or
b,c\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, define

\mathsf{CDH}(V;a,b,c;\mathcal{F}):=\int_{V}\mathsf{CDH}^{c}(x;a,b,c)dx.

(79)

N1:

For
a<0
;
\mathcal{F}
a finite subset of
\mathbb{R}

with size
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
and elements
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\lfloor-a\rfloor}
; and either
b=\bar{c}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with

\textup{Re}(b),\textup{Re}(c)>0
or
b,c\in\mathbb{R}
with
a+b,a+c>0
, define

\mathsf{CDH}(V;a,b,c;\mathcal{F}):=\int_{V}\mathsf{CDH}^{c}(x;a,b,c)dx+\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{F}\cap V}\mathsf{CDH}^{d}(x;a,b,c;\mathcal{F})

(80)

N2:

For
a<0
;
\mathcal{F}
a finite subset of
\mathbb{R}

with size
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
and elements
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\lfloor-a\rfloor}
; and
b,c\in\mathbb{R}
with
b,b+c,c-a>0
and
a+b=-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, define

\mathsf{CDH}(V;a,b,c;\mathcal{F}):=\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{F}\cap V}\mathsf{CDH}^{d}(x;a,b,c;\mathcal{F}).

(81)

Lemma 6.6

In all three cases of Definition
6.5
,
\mathsf{CDH}(\cdot;a,b,c;\mathcal{F})
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

This follows from (3.1), (3.3) and (3.4) in

[
51
]
as a limit when Wilson polynomials
degenerate to dual continuous Hahn polynomials. In particular, this limit corresponds to taking one of the parameters of the Wilson polynomials to infinity. The case when
a=0
does not seem to be covered therein, but can be recovered by taking the limit as
a\to 0
.
∎

We now define what will be the transition probability distribution of the continuous dual Hahn process.

Definition 6.7
.

Assume that
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
with
u+v>0
and
s,t\in[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
with
s<t
.
For any Borel set
V\subset\mathbb{R}
we define the transition probability
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}
as follows. For
m\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}=(0,\infty)

\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,V):=\mathsf{CDH}\left(V;u-\dfrac{t}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{m}}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}-{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{m}}{2};\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}\right).

(82)

If
u-s/2<0
, so that
\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}
, then for
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+s/2\rfloor\rrbracket

\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s),V):=\mathsf{CDH}\left(V;u-\dfrac{t}{2},-u+\dfrac{t}{2}-j,u+\dfrac{t}{2}-s+j;\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}\right).

(83)

If
v+s/2<0
, so that
\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
, then for
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket

\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s),V):=\mathsf{CDH}\left(V;v+j+\dfrac{t}{2},\dfrac{t}{2}-s-v-j,u-\dfrac{t}{2};\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t}\right).

(84)

For all other first arguments besides those described above, we define
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}=0
.

As in Definition
6.2
we introduce the following notation: If
x\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{t}
then we write
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}^{c,c}(x,y)
for the density in
y
of the absolutely continuous part of the measure
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,\cdot)
; If
x\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}
then we write
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}^{c,d}(x,y)
for the mass assigned to
y
of the discrete atomic part of the measure
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,\cdot)
; If
x\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{t}
then we write
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}^{d,c}(x,y)
for the density in
y
of the absolutely continuous part of the measure
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,\cdot)
; If
x\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}
then we write
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}^{d,d}(x,y)
for the mass assigned to
y
of the discrete atomic part of the measure
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,\cdot)
.

In the proof of Lemma
6.9
we will find it convenient to overload notation and write
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,y)
to denote the corresponding density or mass function dictated by whether
x
and
y
are in their discrete or continuous supports (for example, when
x\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,y)=\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}^{c,d}(x,y)
).

The following lemma verifies that
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}
is, indeed, a probability distribution and provides conditions under which the density or mass function is non-zero.

Lemma 6.8

Assume that
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
with
u+v>0
and
s,t\in[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
with
s<t
. For any
x\in\mathcal{S}_{s}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,\cdot)
in Definition
6.7
defines a probability distribution whose support is contained in
\mathcal{S}_{t}
. For all
x\in\mathcal{S}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}_{t}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,y)\geq 0
(we are using the overloaded notation from the end of Definition
6.7
) and the only choices of
x
and
y
for which
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,y)=0
are:
(1)
x\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t}
;
(2)
x\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}
;
(3)
x\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{t}
;
(4)
x\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}
,
y\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t}
;
(5)
x=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}
,
y=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}
with
k>j
.

Proof.

There are three cases to consider: (
82
), (
84
) and (
83
).

In (
82
):
If
u-t/2\geq 0
, then case
P
of Definition
6.5
applies with

a=u-t/2,\quad\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t},\quad b=\dfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{m}}{2},\quad c=\dfrac{t-s}{2}-{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{m}}{2}

since
a\geq 0
,
\mathcal{F}=\varnothing
and
b=\bar{c}
with
\textup{Re}(b)=\dfrac{t-s}{2}>0
; if
u-t/2<0
, then case
N1
of Definition
6.5
applies with the same choices of parameters since
a<0
,
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
and
b=\bar{c}
with
\textup{Re}(b)=\dfrac{t-s}{2}>0
. We see from above that for
x\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
, the measure
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,\cdot)
is supported and everywhere non-zero (in terms of its density or mass function) on
\mathcal{S}^{c}_{t}\cup\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}
. In particular, when
u-t/2\geq 0
and
v+t/2<0
, the mass function
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,y)
is zero on the discrete set
y\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t}
.

In (
83
):
Since
u-s/2<0
, so does
u-t/2<0
. In that case
N2
of Definition
6.5
applies with

a=u-t/2,\quad\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t},\quad b=-u+\dfrac{t}{2}-j,\quad c=u+\dfrac{t}{2}-s+j

since
a<0
,
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
,

b\geq\dfrac{t-s}{2}>0
,
b+c=t-s>0
,
c-a=t-s+j>0
and
a+b=-j
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
We see from this that for
x=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}
, the measure
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,y)
is non-zero only when
y=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t)\in\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}
with
k\in\llbracket 0,j\rrbracket
.

In (
84
):
If
v+j+t/2\geq 0
, then case
P
of Definition
6.5
applies with

a=v+j+t/2,\quad\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t},\quad b=\dfrac{t}{2}-s-v-j,\quad c=u-\dfrac{t}{2}

since
a\geq 0
,
\mathcal{F}=\varnothing
,
b\geq\dfrac{t-s}{2}>0
(since
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
) and
c>0
(since when
v+s/2<0
it follows that
u>0
and hence
t\in[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
implies that
t<2u
); if
v+j+t/2<0
, then case
N1
of Definition
6.5
applies with the same choices of parameters since
a<0
,
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
,
a+b=t-s>0
and
a+c=v+u+j>0
. We see from above that for
x\in\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
, the measure
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,\cdot)
is supported and everywhere non-zero (in terms of its density or mass function) on
\mathcal{S}^{c}_{t}\cup\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t}
. In particular if
u-t/2<0
, the mass function
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(x,y)
is zero on the discrete set
y\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}
.
∎

The next lemma is key to defining the continuous dual Hahn process and to showing that it preserves the class of marginal measures
\mathfrak{p}_{s}
.

Lemma 6.9

Let
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
with
u+v>0
and
0\leq s<t<w<\mathsf{C}_{u,v}
. For any Borel
V\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{p}_{s}(dm)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,V)=\mathfrak{p}_{t}(V),\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,dr)\mathfrak{p}_{t,w}(r,V)=\mathfrak{p}_{s,w}(m,V).

(85)

We prove this after defining the continuous dual Hahn process.

Definition 6.10

(Continuous dual Hahn process)
.

Let
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
with
u+v>0
.
The
continuous dual Hahn process
is the Markov process
\{\mathbb{T}_{s}\}_{s\in[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})}
with time-inhomogeneous state space
\mathcal{S}_{s}
from (
76
) and transition probability given by
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}
. This process is well-defined since Lemma
6.9
shows that Chapman-Kolmogorov is satisfied. Lemma
6.9
also proves that if the continuous dual Hahn process is started at time 0 according to the infinite measure
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
then at time
s\in[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
the marginal infinite measure for the process will be given by
\mathfrak{p}_{s}
.

The preservation of the family
\mathfrak{p}_{s}
should be thought of as similar to the fact that Brownian motion preserves Lebesgue measure.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Lemma
6.9
. We start by recalling
the orthogonality probability measure for the Wilson orthogonal polynomials from
[
51
]
.

Definition 6.11
.

Let
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{C}
either form two conjugate pairs such that

a=\bar{b}
,
c=\bar{d}
, one conjugate pair (either
a=\bar{b}
or

c=\bar{d}
) and one pair of real numbers, or four real numbers; in all
cases assume that
\textup{Re}(b),\textup{Re}(c),\textup{Re}(d)>0
. For such
a,b,c,d
, define

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{c}(x;a,b,c,d)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Gamma(a+b+c+d)\Big\rvert\Gamma\left(a+{i}\frac{\sqrt{x}}{2},b+{i}\frac{\sqrt{x}}{2},c+{i}\frac{\sqrt{x}}{2},d+{i}\frac{\sqrt{x}}{2}\right)\Big\rvert^{2}}{8\pi\cdot\Gamma(a+b,a+c,b+c,a+d,b+d,c+d)\sqrt{x}\,\cdot\,\left\rvert\Gamma\left({i}\sqrt{x}\right)\right\rvert^{2}}.

(86)

Definition 6.12
.

Assume that
a<0
and that
\mathcal{F}
is a finite subset of
\mathbb{R}
with size
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
with elements
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\lfloor-a\rfloor}
. Assume that
b,c,d
and
j
satisfy one of the three conditions:

•

b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
c=\bar{d}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with

\textup{Re}(c),\textup{Re}(d)>0
, and
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-a\rfloor\rrbracket
;

•

b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, with
a+d,a+c>0
,
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-a\rfloor\rrbracket
;

•

b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}
with
b,b+c,c-a>0,

a+b=-k
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-a\rfloor\rrbracket
.

For
a,b,c,d
,
\mathcal{F}
, and
j
as above, define

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{d}(x_{j};a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F}):=\dfrac{\left[2a,a+b,a+c,a+d\right]_{j}\cdot(a+j)}{\left[1,a-b+1,a-c+1,a-d+1\right]_{j}\cdot a}

(87)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma(a+b+c+d,b-a,c-a,d-a)}{\Gamma(-2a,b+c,c+d,b+d)}.

(88)

For all other first arguments, define
\mathsf{W}^{d}(x_{j};a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F})=0
.

Definition 6.13
.

We will define the measure
\mathsf{W}(V;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F})
(for Borel subsets
V
of
\mathbb{R}
) under four possible sets of conditions on parameters:

P1:

For
a\geq 0
;
\mathcal{F}=\varnothing
;
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
; and either
c=\bar{d}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with

\textup{Re}(c),\textup{Re}(d)>0
or
c,d\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, define

\mathsf{W}(V;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F}):=\int_{V}\mathsf{W}^{c}(x;a,b,c,d)dx.

(89)

P2:

For
a=\bar{b}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with
\textup{Re}(a),\textup{Re}(b)>0
;
\mathcal{F}=\varnothing
; and either
c=\bar{d}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with

\textup{Re}(c),\textup{Re}(d)>0
or
c,d\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, define

\mathsf{W}(V;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F}):=\int_{V}\mathsf{W}^{c}(x;a,b,c,d)dx.

(90)

N1:

For
a<0
;
\mathcal{F}
a finite subset of
\mathbb{R}

with size
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
and elements
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\lfloor-a\rfloor}
;
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
; and either
c=\bar{d}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
with
\textup{Re}(c),\textup{Re}(d)>0
or
c,d\in\mathbb{R}
with
a+c,a+d>0
, define

\mathsf{W}(V;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F}):=\int_{V}\mathsf{W}^{c}(x;a,b,c,d)dx+\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{F}\cap V}\mathsf{W}^{d}(x;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F})

(91)

N2:

For
a<0
;
\mathcal{F}
a finite subset of
\mathbb{R}

with size
|\mathcal{F}|=\lfloor-a\rfloor+1
and elements
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\lfloor-a\rfloor}
;
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
a+b=-k
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
; and
c,d\in\mathbb{R}
with
b+c,b+d,c-a,d-a>0
, define

\mathsf{W}(V;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F}):=\sum\limits_{x\in\mathcal{F}\cap V}\mathsf{W}^{d}(x;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F}).

(92)

Lemma 6.14

In all cases of Definition
6.13
,
\mathsf{W}(\cdot;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F})
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the identities (3.1), (3.3) and (3.4) in
[
51
]
. The case when
a=0
does not seem to be covered therein, but can be recovered by taking the limit as
a\to 0
.
∎

We can now give the proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Proof of Lemma
6.9
.

The idea is to rewrite the relations in Lemma
6.9
in terms of the
\mathsf{CDH}(\cdot;a,b,c;\mathcal{F})
and
\mathsf{W}(\cdot;a,b,c,d;\mathcal{F})
measures, and then use the fact that they integrate to 1 to demonstrate the desired identities.

Proving the first identity in Lemma
6.9
.
It suffices to prove that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{p}_{s}(dm)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)=\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)

(93)

for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Here we have overloaded the
\mathfrak{p}_{t}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}
notation (as density and mass functions) as explained at the end of Definitions
6.2
and
6.7
. For
r\notin\mathcal{S}_{t}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)=0
. Likewise, for such
r
it is easy to see from the five cases in Lemma
6.8
that the left-hand side in (
93
) is also zero. Thus, we assume now that
r\in\mathcal{S}_{t}
.
We can now divide and rewrite (
93
) as

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(dm)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=1.

(94)

To show this identity, we identify the integrand above with the continuous dual Hahn probability measure (hence its integral is 1). There are three cases which we address below.

Case 1.

For
r\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{t}
,

\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(dm)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=\mathsf{CDH}\left(dm;v+\dfrac{s}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{r}}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}-{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{r}}{2};\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}\right).

(95)

To prove this, observe that for
m\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
we may rewrite

\dfrac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(m)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=\mathsf{CDH}^{c}\left(m;v+\dfrac{s}{2};\dfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{r}}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}-{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{r}}{2}\right).

(96)

The computation here is obtained by regrouping the terms and
using the idenity
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x).

Similarly, for
m=\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
,

\dfrac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(m)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=\mathsf{CDH}^{d}\left(m;v+\dfrac{s}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{r}}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}-{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{r}}{2};\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}\right).

(97)

From Lemma
6.8
we have that for
m=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)=0
.
Depending on the value of
v
, we see that the parameters in (
96
) and (
97
) either satisfy
P
or
N1
in Definition
6.5
and either way we arrive at (
95
) and verify that the right-hand side is a probability measure.

Case 2.

For
r=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}
,

\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(dm)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=\mathsf{CDH}\left(dm;u+k-\dfrac{s}{2},v+\dfrac{s}{2},-u-k+t-\dfrac{s}{2};\mathcal{S}^{d,u}_{s}\right).

(98)

To prove this, observe that for
m\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
we may rewrite

\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(dm)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=\mathsf{CDH}^{c}\left(m;u+k-\dfrac{s}{2},v+\dfrac{s}{2},-u-k+t-\dfrac{s}{2}\right).

(99)

Similarly, for
m=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(t)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
,

\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(dm)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=\mathsf{CDH}^{d}\left(m;u+k-\dfrac{s}{2},v+\dfrac{s}{2},-u-k+t-\dfrac{s}{2};\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}\right).

(100)

From Lemma
6.8
we have that for
m=\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)=0
.
Depending on the value of
u
, the parameters in (
99
) and (
100
) either satisfy
P
or
N1
in Definition
6.5
and either way we arrive at (
98
) and verify that the right-hand side is a probability measure.

Case 3.

For
r=\mathbf{v}^{v}_{k}(t)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{t}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t}
,

\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(dm)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=\mathsf{CDH}\left(dm;v\dfrac{s}{2},-v-\dfrac{s}{2}-k,v+t-\dfrac{s}{2}+k;\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}\right).

(101)

To prove this, observe that for
m=\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
we may rewrite

\dfrac{\mathfrak{p}_{s}(m)\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)}{\mathfrak{p}_{t}(r)}=\mathsf{CDH}^{d}\left(m;v+\dfrac{s}{2},-v-\dfrac{s}{2}-k,v+t-\dfrac{s}{2}+k;\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}\right).

(102)

From Lemma
6.8
we have that for
m=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)=0
and likewise for
m\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,r)=0
.
The parameters in (
102
) satisfy
N2
in Definition
6.5
and thus we arrive at (
101
) and verify that the right-hand side is a probability measure.

Proving the second identity in Lemma
6.9
.
It suffices to prove that

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,dr)\mathfrak{p}_{t,w}(r,x)=\mathfrak{p}_{s,w}(m,x)

(103)

for all
m,x\in\mathbb{R}
. As in the proof of the first identity, we are
overloading the
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}
notation (as density and mass functions) as
explained at the end of Definition
6.7
. If
m\notin\mathcal{S}_{s}
or
x\notin\mathcal{S}_{t}
then
\mathfrak{p}_{s,w}(m,x)=0
. It is
likewise easy to see that in this case, the right-hand side of
(
103
) is also zero. The five cases in Lemma

6.8
identify the choices of
m\in\mathcal{S}_{s}
and
x\in\mathcal{S}_{t}
for which we still have
\mathfrak{p}_{s,w}(m,x)=0
. It is easy to
check from that list that for these choices of
m
and
x
, the right-hand side of (
103
) is also zero. Thus, we may now assume that
m
and
x
are such that
\mathfrak{p}_{s,w}(m,x)>0
.

In that case, we may divide and rewrite (
103
) as

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,dr)\mathfrak{p}_{t,w}(r,x)}{\mathfrak{p}_{s,w}(m,x)}=1.

To show this identity, we identify the integrand above with the Wilson probability measure (hence its integral is 1). There are five cases which we address below. In light of the five cases in Lemma
6.8
, we see that these are the only cases in which
\mathfrak{p}_{s,w}(m,x)>0
. Since the proofs hear are similar to those used to show the first identity in Lemma
6.9
, we simply record the five cases. To shorten notation, write
(\star):=\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}(m,dr)\mathfrak{p}_{t,w}(r,x)}{\mathfrak{p}_{s,w}(m,x)}
. Then

Case 1.
For
m\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
and
x\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{w}
,

(\star)=\mathsf{W}\left(dr;\dfrac{w-t}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{x}}{2},\dfrac{w-t}{2}-{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{x}}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{m}}{2},\dfrac{t-s}{2}-{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{m}}{2}\right).

(104)

P2
in Definition
6.13
applies.

Case 2.
For
m\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}
and
x=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(w)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{w}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{w}
,

(\star)=\mathsf{W}\left(dr;u+k-\dfrac{t}{2},w-\dfrac{t}{2}-u-k,\dfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{m}}{2};\dfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{m}}{2};\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}\right).

(105)

Depending on the value of
u
, either
P1
or
N1
in Definition
6.13
applies.

Case 3.
For
m=\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
and
x\in\mathcal{S}^{c}_{w}
,

(\star)=\mathsf{W}\left(dr;v+j+\dfrac{t}{2},-v+\dfrac{t}{2}-s-j,\dfrac{w-t}{2}+{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{x}}{2},\dfrac{w-t}{2}-{i}\dfrac{\sqrt{x}}{2};\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t}\right).

(106)

Depending on the value of
v
, either

P1
or
N1
in Definition
6.13
applies.

Case 4.
For
m=\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{s}
and
x=\mathbf{v}^{v}_{k}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{w}=\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{w}
,

(\star)=\mathsf{W}\left(dr;v+k+\dfrac{t}{2},-v-\dfrac{t}{2}-j,-v+\dfrac{t}{2}-k-s,v-\dfrac{t}{2}+j+w;\mathcal{SV}^{d,v}_{t}\right).

(107)

N2
in Definition
6.13
applies.

Case 5.
For
m=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{s}
and
x=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(w)\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{w}=\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{w}
with
k\in\llbracket 0,j\rrbracket
,

(\star)=\mathsf{W}\left(dr;u-\dfrac{t}{2}+k,-u+\dfrac{t}{2}-j,-u-\dfrac{t}{2}-k+w,u+\dfrac{t}{2}+j-s;\mathcal{SU}^{d,u}_{t}\right).

(108)

N2
in Definition
6.13
applies.
∎

7
Asymptotics of the ASEP generating function and proof of Theorem
1.2
(
5
)

The main technical ingredient to proving Theorem
1.2
(
5
) is provided by Proposition
7.1
which is stated below and proved in Section
8
. Before stating the proposition, we rewrite the function
\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
in (
28
) as in Section
2
as

\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\frac{\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})}{\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{0},\vec{X})},\quad\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X}):=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{G}\Big((\mathbb{T}_{s_{1}},\ldots,\mathbb{T}_{s_{d+1}});\vec{c};\vec{X}\Big)\right],

(109)

where
\mathbb{T}
is the continuous dual Hahn process started with
\mathbb{T}_{0}
distributed according to the infinite measure
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
. Explicitly, this means that

\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\int\mathcal{G}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathfrak{p}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},dr_{i})\cdot\mathfrak{p}_{s_{d+1}}(dr_{d+1})

where
\mathfrak{p}_{s}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{s,t}
(recall Definitions
6.2
and
6.7
) are the marginal and transition measures for the continuous dual Hahn process with this initial distribution (recall Definition
6.10
), and the function
\mathcal{G}
is defined as

\mathcal{G}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X}):=\exp\left(\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{d+1}(s_{k}^{2}-r_{k})(X_{k}-X_{k-1})\right).

(110)

Recall that
\vec{s}=(s_{1}>\cdots>s_{d+1})
is related to
\vec{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})
as in (
25
) by
s_{k}=c_{k}+\cdots+c_{d}
for
k\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
and
s_{d+1}=0
. In (
109
) we are considering transition from
r_{i+1}
to
r_{i}
between times
s_{i+1}
and
s_{i}
(recall
s_{i+1}<s_{i}
). This slightly odd labeling of time (and hence of the
r
variables) comes from a time reversal in the Askey-Wilson process which produces the continuous dual Hahn process.
For
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
define
\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v}:=\frac{1}{d}\mathsf{C}_{u,v}
where
\mathsf{C}_{u,v}
is defined in (
20
).
The following result is proved in Section
8
.

Proposition 7.1

Assume that
q,A,B,C
and
D
satisfy Assumption
1.1
and
are parameterized by
N
and
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
with
u+v>0
; let

H^{(N)}_{u,v}
be the random function in
C([0,1])
defined in (
18
) whose law
\mu^{(N)}_{u,v}
is that of the diffusively scaled open ASEP height function stationary measure with the above specified parameters. For
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let
\vec{X},\vec{c}
and
\vec{s}
be as in (
25
) and let
\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
denote the Laplace transform of
H^{(N)}_{u,v}
defined in (
31
) with
\tilde{c}=0
.
Then, for all
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
, we have the point-wise convergence of the open ASEP Laplace transform

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X}).

(111)

Remark 7.2
.

The point-wise convergence in (
111
) is only stated for
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
. Here we explain why. The Askey-Wilson process marginal and transition probability distribution involves a mixture of an absolutely continuous and discrete atomic part. The nature of the atomic part depends on the number of parameters whose norm exceeds
1
. By limiting the range of the
c_{k}
we limit which atoms can arise. If we permitted the
c_{k}
to be larger, we would need to keep track of additional groups of atoms as well as transition probabilities between them which would increase the complexity of notation and require additional care. For our purposes it is sufficient that we have convergence on some open interval.

There is an alternative approach to minimize the contribution of atoms. (This possible approach came out in discussions with Yizao Wang, after communicating a draft of our paper to him.) We could utilize the more general formula in Corollary
2.2
for
\phi^{(N)}(\vec{c},\tilde{c},\vec{X})
and choose
\vec{c}
and
\tilde{c}
so that
-2v<s_{k}+\tilde{c}<2u
(the interval
(-2v,2u)
is non-empty since
u+v>0
). This avoids atoms coming from the
a
and
c
terms in (
129
) but may introduce atoms coming from the
b
and
d
terms in that equation. These atoms are located near
-1
and it seems that their contribution will disappear in our scaling limit. We do not pursue it here.

Let us briefly compare our proof below to the style of proof used in
[
18
]
. Therein, the authors used the fact that their limiting
Laplace transform formula could be identified as the Laplace transform for a bona-fide probability measure (this identification is made in

[
15
]
). On account of this, the authors are able to apply a result which generalizes
[
22
]
and shows that convergence
of the Laplace transform on any open set to a Laplace transform of some other probability measure implies weak convergence of the underlying probability measures to that limiting measure. If we wanted to apply this exact approach in our current situation we would need to know a priori that
\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
is the Laplace transform for some probability measure. When we first posted this paper, such an identification was an open problem, hence we came up with another approach. Since first posting this paper, this fact has been established in
[
14
]
when
\min(u,v)>-1
(see also
[
8
]
). Since the restriction on
\min(u,v)>-1
does not cover the full range of
u+v>0
, we still provide our approach which does not rely on the identification of the limit as the Laplace transform of a probability measure. Our approach uses some probabilistic information about the WASEP process, namely tightness and uniform control over exponential moments (both of which follow from a nice coupling of the stationary measure with random walks) to show that
\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
coincides on an open set with the Laplace transform of some sub(sub)sequential weak limits. This identifies uniquely the weak limits along all subsubsequences as being the same, and hence shows convergence of the original sequence of measures.

Using Proposition
7.1
we may now give the proof of Theorem
1.2
(
5
).

Proof of Theorem
1.2
(
5
).

There are two things to show here. The first is that when
u+v>0
, the tight sequence of measures
\mu^{(N)}_{u,v}
(i.e. the laws of
H^{(N)}_{u,v}(\cdot)\in C([0,1])
) from Theorem
1.2
(
1
) has a unique limit point. To show this it suffices to show that the finite dimensional distributions of
H^{(N)}_{u,v}(\cdot)
converge weakly. The second is to show is that the Laplace transform of the limiting finite dimensional distributions are given by
\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
as claimed in (
28
).

Fix any
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
0<X_{1}<\cdots<X_{d}\leq 1
, and let
X^{(N)}=N^{-1}\lfloor NX\rfloor
. We will consider the sequence of random vectors
H^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{X}):=\big(H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X^{(N)}_{1}),\ldots,H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X^{(N)}_{d})\big)
and use
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}
to denote the law of the corresponding random vector. For
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
we let
L^{(N)}(\vec{c}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{\vec{c}\cdot\vec{x}}\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(d\vec{x})
denote the Laplace transform of
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}
. Note that
L^{(N)}(\vec{c})=\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\langle e^{-\sum_{k=1}^{d}c_{k}H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X^{(N)}_{k})}\rangle_{N}
.

Theorem
1.2
(
1
) shows that
\{\mathbb{P}^{(N)}\}_{N}
is a tight sequence as
N\to\infty
. In particular, for any
subsequence
N_{k}
this implies that there exists a further subsubsequence
N_{k_{j}}
along which the
\mathbb{P}^{(N_{k_{j}})}
converge weakly to a limit which we will denote by
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
. A priori,
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
may depend on the choice of subsequence and subsubsequence. We will show that it does not. This will imply that the original sequence
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}
converges weakly to
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
as well.

Proposition
7.1
shows that there exists an open interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
(e.g.
I=(0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
works) so that
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}L^{(N)}(\vec{c})=\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
for all
\vec{c}\in I^{d}
. This convergence, of course, extends to the subsubsequence
N_{k_{j}}
. We claim that the Laplace transform
L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{\vec{c}\cdot\vec{x}}\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}(d\vec{x})

of
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
is finite for all
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
and that for
\vec{c}\in I^{d}
it agrees with
\phi
so that
L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c})=\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
. Let us assume this claim for the moment. Then by analyticity of
L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c})
we see that the knowledge of
\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
for
\vec{c}\in I^{d}
uniquely (by uniqueness of analytic continuations) characterizes the Laplace transform elsewhere, including on the imaginary axis. On account of this and the Cramer-Wold device, we can uniquely characterize the law of
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
from
\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
. Since the same
\phi
arises for any choice of subsubsequence
N_{k_{j}}
, this implies that
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
does not depend on the choice of subsubsequence and hence that
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}
converges weakly to
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
which has Laplace transform
L^{(\infty)}
which coincides with
\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
for
\vec{c}\in I^{d}
. Finally, note that we have been dealing above with convergence of
\big(H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X^{(N)}_{1}),\ldots,H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X^{(N)}_{d})\big)
. This also implies that
\big(H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X_{1}),\ldots,H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X_{d})\big)
converges weakly to the same limit since
|H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X^{(N)})-H^{(N)}_{u,v}(X)|<N^{-1/2}
.

What remains from above is to prove the claim that
L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c})
is finite for all
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
and that
L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c})=\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
for
\vec{c}\in I^{d}
. To prove the first part of this claim we appeal to (
29
) (which follows from Theorem
1.2
(
2
) and (
3
)) which implies that if
\big(H_{u,v}(X_{1}),\ldots,H_{u,v}(X_{d})\big)
has distribution
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
then there exists a coupling of that random vector w with
B_{-v}
, a standard Brownian motions of drift
-v
, and with
B_{u}
, a standard Brownian motion of drift
u
, such that
B_{-v}(X_{k})\leq H_{u,v}(X_{k})\leq B_{u}(X_{k})
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
. Owing to this and the Gaussian tails of Brownian motion, it follows easily that the Laplace transform
L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c})
is finite for all
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
.

Finally, we claim that
\lim_{N\to\infty}L^{(N)}(\vec{c})=L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c})
for all
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
. From this it will immediately follow that
L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c})=\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
for
\vec{c}\in I^{d}
. To show the claim it suffices to show the following: For all
{\varepsilon}>0
there exists
M>0
such that for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{1}\{||\vec{x}||>M\}\,e^{\vec{c}\cdot\vec{x}}\,\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(d\vec{x})<{\varepsilon},

(112)

where
||\vec{x}||=\max(|x_{1}|,\ldots,|x_{d}|)
.
By convergence of
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}
to
\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{1}\{||\vec{x}||\leq M\}\,e^{\vec{c}\cdot\vec{x}}\,\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(d\vec{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{1}\{||\vec{x}||\leq M\}\,e^{\vec{c}\cdot\vec{x}}\,\mathbb{P}^{(\infty)}(d\vec{x})

for all
M>0
.
Combining this with the error bound claimed in (
112
) proves that
\lim_{N\to\infty}L^{(N)}(\vec{c})=L^{(\infty)}(\vec{c})
.

To prove (
112
) we use Cauchy-Schwarz to show that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbf{1}\{||\vec{x}||>M\}\,e^{\vec{c}\cdot\vec{x}}\,\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(d\vec{x})\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(||\vec{x}||>M)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\vec{c}\cdot\vec{x}}\,\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(d\vec{x})}.

By tightness we know that for any
{\varepsilon}>0
there is some
M>0
so that for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
,
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(||\vec{x}||>M)<{\varepsilon}
. So, it suffices to show that the other term on the right-hand side stays uniformly bounded in
N
. Notice that by repeated use of Hölder’s inequality we can bound

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{2\vec{c}\cdot\vec{x}}\,\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(d\vec{x})\leq\prod_{k=1}^{d}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2dc_{k}x_{k}}\,\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(dx_{k})\right)^{1/d},

where we are writing
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}(dx_{k})
for the marginal of
\mathbb{P}^{(N)}
in the
x_{k}
coordinate.
The integrals on the right-hand side above can be rewritten in terms of the notation of Proposition
4.2
as

\mathbb{E}[Z^{(N)}_{u,v}(X_{k})^{2dc_{k}}]
. For
c_{k}>0
, we can bound
\mathbb{E}[Z^{(N)}_{u,v}(X_{k})^{2dc_{k}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[Z^{(N)}_{u,v}(X_{k})^{n}]+1
where
n
is any integer which is larger than
2dc_{k}
; for
c_{k}<0
, we can similarly bound
\mathbb{E}[Z^{(N)}_{u,v}(X_{k})^{2dc_{k}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[Z^{(N)}_{u,v}(X_{k})^{n}]+1
where
n
is any integer which is smaller than
2dc_{k}
. In either case, we can uniformly bound
\mathbb{E}[Z^{(N)}_{u,v}(X_{k})^{n}]
via the bound (
49
) in Proposition
4.2
(
5
). This proves (
112
).
∎

We close this section by recording one of the results proved above that generalizes (
111
) to all
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
.

Lemma 7.3

For all
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
,
\lim_{N\to\infty}\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
.

8
Proof of Proposition
7.1

We start, in Section
8.1
, with a heuristic explanation for the convergence in Proposition
7.1
. In Section
8.2
we introduce scalings of our Askey-Wilson process formulas in a manner fitting for asymptotics. Section
8.3
contains precise bounds and asymptotic results involving these scaled Askey-Wilson process formulas (these are proved later in Section
8.5
). Section
8.4
puts these bounds and asymptotics together to prove the convergence in (
111
)—thus proving Proposition
7.1
. The key technical input to the asymptotics performed in this section are the
q
-Pochhammer asymptotics from Proposition
2.3
(which are proved in Section
9
).

8.1
Heuristic for the convergence (
111
)

Corollary
2.2
provides a formula, (
31
), for
\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
in terms of a ratio of expectations over the Askey-Wilson process. In the numerator of this ratio, there is a product over
d+1
terms which take the form (assume
X_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}/N
for the moment)

\left(\cosh\left(s_{k}/\sqrt{N}\right)+\mathbb{Y}_{e^{-2s_{k}/\sqrt{N}}}\right)^{N(X_{k}-X_{k-1})}.

(113)

As
N\to\infty
, we are taking the
s_{k}
to be fixed and positive, and
likewise for the difference
X_{k}-X_{k-1}
. As
N\to\infty
, we have that
\cosh(s_{k}/\sqrt{N})\approx 1+\tfrac{s_{k}^{2}}{2N}
. The question is how does
\mathbb{Y}_{e^{-2s_{k}/\sqrt{N}}}
behave. Recall that from Section
6.1
there are two parts to the support of the Askey-Wilson process
\mathbb{Y}_{s}
, an absolutely continuous part of support
\mathrm{S}^{c}_{s}=[-1,1]
and a discrete atomic part
\mathrm{S}^{d}_{s}
support above
1
. In our scaling, the atomic part lives in a
N^{-1}
window above
1
. Thus, writing
\mathbb{Y}_{e^{-2s/\sqrt{N}}}=1-\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{s}^{(N)}/(2N)
and assuming that
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{s}^{(N)}
is of order one, (
113
) behaves (for
N
large enough) like
2^{N(X_{k}-X_{k-1})}
times

e^{\frac{1}{4}(s^{2}-\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{s}^{(N)})(X_{k}-X_{k-1})}
. This is the origin of the
\mathcal{G}
function in (
109
).

There are a few issues complicating the above heuristic. Recall that in (
31
) we are considering the Askey-Wilson process
\mathbb{Y}_{s}
with marginal distribution
\pi_{s}
. Under our scalings, while the discrete part of
\mathbb{Y}_{e^{-2s_{k}/\sqrt{N}}}
does converge to a limit in a
N^{-1}
window above
1
, the absolutely continuous part does not stay in that window. In fact, it remains of full support in
\mathrm{S}^{c}=[-1,1]
even though the window is of order
N^{-1}
around
1
. However, the
\mathcal{G}
function has strong decay as the
\mathbb{Y}_{e^{-2s_{k}/\sqrt{N}}}
variable drops below a
N^{-1}
window of
1
. Thus, we need to justify that the contribution to the expectation coming from
\mathbb{Y}_{e^{-2s_{k}/\sqrt{N}}}
below this window is negligible in the large
N
limit. Furthermore, we need to determine what happens to
\mathbb{Y}_{e^{-2s_{k}/\sqrt{N}}}
when we only consider it in this window. This leads to the continuous dual Hahn process that we have introduced in Section
6.2
. (The continuous dual Hahn process can be thought of as a
tangent
process to the Askey-Wilson process.)
We will see that the marginal distribution in this
N^{-1}
window has
a limit when compensated by a suitable power of
N
. The limit is no longer a probability measure, but rather of infinite mass. However, the transition probabilities of the Askey-Wilson process converge to bona-fide transition probabilities.

8.2
Rewriting formulas to take asymptotics

Recall that
q,A,B,C
and
D
satisfy Assumption
1.1
and are parameterized by
N
(through
q=e^{-2/\sqrt{N}}
) and
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
with
u+v>0
. In what follows we will assume that our Askey-Wilson processes
\mathbb{Y}
depend
N
,
u
and
v
through these parameters
q,A,B,C,D
. As
N
changes, the law of the process changes. Though this dependence will be implicit at times, it should not be forgotten.

We will assume here and below that the Askey-Wilson process
\mathbb{Y}_{s}
is always taken with marginal distribution
\pi_{s}
for all
s
.
Define the centered and scaled Askey-Wilson process

\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{s}^{(N)}:=2N\left(1-\mathbb{Y}_{q^{s}}\right).

(114)

Due to the factor
q^{s}=e^{-2s/\sqrt{N}}
, the process
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{s}^{(N)}
involves a time reversal of
\mathbb{Y}
. Thus, its transition probabilities
involve a conjugation by the marginal distribution. In writing down the marginal distribution and transition probabilities of
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}^{(N)}

we distinguish the absolutely continuous and discrete atomic part of the support and measure. This is important since there is a Jacobian
factor which is present when the measure is absolutely continuous, though not when it is discrete.

Remark 8.1
.

This time reversal, which was also used in
[
16
]
, is convenient conceptually since it allows us to write out limiting formulas in terms of a process that moves forward in time. It is not strictly necessary, though. We have opted to include it since it more closely matches
[
16
]
.

For any Borel
V\subset\mathbb{R}
, denote the marginal probability that
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{s}^{(N)}\in V
by
\hat{\pi}^{(N)}_{s}(V)
. This probability measure can be written as the sum of an absolutely continuous part and a discrete atomic part. We denote the density of the absolutely continuous part by
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{s}(y)
and the probability mass of the discrete atomic part by
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{s}(y)
. The support of
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{s}
is

\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}_{s}=\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}:=[0,4N]

and does not depend on
s
. The support of the discrete atomic part is

\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}:=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}:1-\tfrac{y}{2N}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}_{q^{s}}\right\}

where
\mathrm{S}^{d}_{q^{s}}
is defined via (
66
) with
A,B,C,D
and
q
scaled dependent on
N
and
u
and
v
as in the statement of Proposition
7.1
. We will use
\pi^{(N),c}_{s}
and
\pi^{(N),d}_{s}
to denote
\pi^{c}_{s}
and
\pi^{d}_{s}
from (
68
) where
A,B,C,D
and
q
are scaled dependent on
N
and
u
and
v
as noted above. Similarly, we introduce a superscript
(N)
for the transition probabilities defined in (
67
).

For any
s
the marginal distribution of
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{s}^{(N)}
is specified by
(
y\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}
in the first formula and
y\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
in the second)

\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(y)=\tfrac{1}{2N}\pi^{(N),c}_{q^{t}}\left(1-\tfrac{y}{2N}\right),\qquad\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(y)=\pi^{(N),d}_{q^{t}}\left(1-\tfrac{y}{2N}\right).

(115)

Using the same convention as described below (
67
), for

x\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}
we write
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(x,y)
for
the transition probability density supported on
y\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}

while
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}(x,y)
is the mass function for

y\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
. Similarly, for
x\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
we
write
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(x,y)
for the transition
probability density supported on
y\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}
while
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}(x,y)
is the mass function for
y\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
. For all other values of
x
or
y
, we declare
these functions to be zero. With this notation we have

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2N}\pi^{(N),c,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{y}{2N},1-\tfrac{x}{2N}\right)\cdot\dfrac{\pi^{(N),c}_{q^{t}}\left(1-\frac{y}{2N}\right)}{\pi^{(N),c}_{q^{s}}\left(1-\frac{x}{2N}\right)}

(116)

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2N}\pi^{(N),c,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{y}{2N},1-\tfrac{x}{2N}\right)\cdot\dfrac{\pi^{(N),c}_{q^{t}}\left(1-\frac{y}{2N}\right)}{\pi^{(N),d}_{q^{s}}\left(1-\frac{x}{2N}\right)}

(117)

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\pi^{(N),d,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{y}{2N},1-\tfrac{x}{2N}\right)\cdot\dfrac{\pi^{(N),d}_{q^{t}}\left(1-\frac{y}{2N}\right)}{\pi^{(N),c}_{q^{s}}\left(1-\frac{x}{2N}\right)}

(118)

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{y}{2N},1-\tfrac{x}{2N}\right)\cdot\dfrac{\pi^{(N),d}_{q^{t}}\left(1-\frac{y}{2N}\right)}{\pi^{(N),d}_{q^{s}}\left(1-\frac{x}{2N}\right)}.

(119)

We need one last piece of notation. For
\vec{X},\vec{c}
and

\vec{s}
as in (
25
) and
\vec{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{d+1})

define

\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X}):={\bf 1}_{\vec{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\leq 4N}}\cdot 2^{-N}\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}\left(\cosh\left(\tfrac{s_{k}}{\sqrt{N}}\right)+1-\tfrac{r_{k}}{2N}\right)^{N(X^{(N)}_{k}-X^{(N)}_{k-1})}.

(120)

Recall that
X^{(N)}=N^{-1}\lfloor NX\rfloor
.
Now, we can rewrite (
31
) as in (
109
):

\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\frac{\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})}{\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{0},\vec{X})},\quad\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X}):=\mathbb{E}\left[N^{u+v}\mathcal{G}^{(N)}\Big((\mathbb{\hat{Y}}^{(N)}_{s_{1}},\ldots,\mathbb{\hat{Y}}^{(N)}_{s_{d+1}});\vec{c};\vec{X}\Big)\right],

(121)

or more explicitly,

\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\int\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})\prod_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\pi}^{(N)}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},dr_{i})\cdot N^{u+v}\hat{\pi}^{(N)}_{s_{d+1}}(dr_{d+1}).

(122)

The inclusion of the factor
N^{u+v}
will be seen below as necessary to have
\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
converge to a limit as
N\to\infty
(in particular, it is required in Lemma
8.3
for the convergence of the marginal distribution to a non-trivial limit). Since
\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
is a ratio of such terms, its inclusion in both the numerator and denominator does not change the ratio.

8.3
Lemmas for asymptotics and bounds

We provide the key technical results necessary to prove the point-wise convergence in (
111
). The proofs of these lemmas are postponed until Section
8.5
. We assume the scalings in Proposition
7.1
and the notation introduced in Section
8.2
.

Equation (
122
) is our starting point for asymptotics. In order to take
N\to\infty
therein, we must control the convergence of
\mathcal{G}^{(N)}
(in terms of a point-wise limit and dominating function) and the convergence of the
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}
process. We start with the
\mathcal{G}^{(N)}
function.

Lemma 8.2

For every compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\vec{c}\in I^{d}
and
all
\vec{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
,

\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})\leq C\prod\limits_{k=1}^{d+1}e^{-(X_{k}-X_{k-1})\frac{r_{k}}{4}+\frac{|r_{k}|}{2N}}

(123)

where
\vec{X}
and
\vec{c}
are as in (
25
).
For all
\vec{r}\,^{(N)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
if
\lim_{N\to\infty}\vec{r}\,^{(N)}=\vec{r}
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r}\,^{(N)};\vec{c};\vec{X})=\mathcal{G}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X}).

(124)

The next four lemmas provide limits with quantified error bounds for the marginal and transition measures of
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}
. These limits are written in terms of the continuous dual Hahn process transition measures and family of marginal distributions from Definitions
6.7
and
6.2
. The first lemma deals with the continuous part
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}
of the distribution of
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{t}
, and the second with the discrete part.

Lemma 8.3

For all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and all
\eta>1
, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
r\in[0,4N]
,

N^{u+v}\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\,\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)=\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}(r)\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)},

(125)

where the error term satisfy

\Big|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)\Big|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(126)

For each fixed
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
we have the point-wise convergence

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{u+v}\,\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)=\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}(r).

(127)

The next lemma deals with the atomic part
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}
of
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}_{t}
. We show that the locations and masses of the finitely many atoms have limits as
N\to\infty
and that those match with
\mathfrak{p}^{d}_{t}
from Definition
6.2
.

Recall from Section
8.2
that we write
\pi^{(N),d}_{t}
to denote the atomic measure
\pi^{d}_{t}
from (
68
).
In light of (
68
) and (
62
), for any Borel set
V\subset\mathbb{R}

\pi^{(N),d}_{t}(V)=\sum\limits_{y\in V\cap\mathrm{S}^{d}(a,b,c,d,q)}AW^{d}(y;a,b,c,d,q)

(128)

where
q=e^{-2/\sqrt{N}}
and

a=q^{v+t/2},\quad b=-q^{1+t/2},\quad c=q^{u-t/2},\quad d=-q^{1-t/2}.

(129)

If
t\in(-2,2)
then
|b|,|d|<1
. Since
ac=q^{u+v}<1
, it follows that at most one of
|a|
or
|c|
can exceed
1
.

When
u-t/2<0
,
|c|>1
and the set of atoms in
\pi^{(N),d}_{t}
are given by

\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{j}(t):=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(q^{u+j-t/2}+q^{-(u+j-t/2)}\right),\quad j\in\llbracket 0,\ldots\lfloor-u+t/2\rfloor\rrbracket.

(130)

Similarly, when

v+t/2<0
,
|a|>1
and thus based on the discussion prior to (
63
), we conclude that the set of atoms in
\pi^{(N),d}_{t}
are given by

\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(t):=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(q^{v+j+t/2}+q^{-(v+j+t/2)}\right),\quad\textrm{for }j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-t/2\rfloor\rrbracket.

(131)

These account for all of the atoms in
\mathrm{S}^{d}(a,b,c,d,q)
.
Finally, let us denote

\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(t):=-2N\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{j}(t)-1\right),\quad\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(t):=-2N\left(\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(t)-1\right).

(132)

The support,
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
, of
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}
is the union of these atoms. At most one type of atoms, either from
u
or
v
, will appear for a given
t
. As
q
varies with
N
, the number and type of atoms remains fixed.

Lemma 8.4

Assume
t\in(-2,2)
. The location and masses of the (finitely many) atoms of
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}
converge to those of
\mathfrak{p}^{d}_{t}
(recall (
70
)). Explicitly, when
v+t/2<0
the atoms of
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}
are at
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(t)
for
j\in\llbracket 0,\ldots\lfloor-v-t/2\rfloor\rrbracket
and

\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(t)=\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(t):=-4(v+j+t/2)^{2}.

(133)

Similarly, when
u-t/2<0
the atoms of
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}
are at
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(t)
for
j\in\llbracket 0,\ldots\lfloor u-t/2\rfloor\rrbracket
and

\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(t)=\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(t):=-4(u+j+t/2)^{2}.

(134)

There exists
C,\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and all atoms

\begin{split}N^{u+v}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(t))&=\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{d}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(t))\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d}_{t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(t))},\\
N^{u+v}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(t))&=\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{d}(\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(t))\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d}_{t}(\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(t))}\end{split}

(135)

where the error terms satisfy

\Big|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d}_{t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(t))\Big|,\Big|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d}_{t}(\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(t))\Big|\leq CN^{-\chi}.

(136)

In particular,

\begin{split}\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{u+v}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(t))&=\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{d}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(t)),\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{u+v}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(t))&=\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{d}(\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(t)).\end{split}

(137)

The next two lemmas deal with the transition probabilities for
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}
. The first of these lemmas deals with the continuous part of the transition probability while the second deals with the discrete part.

Lemma 8.5

For all real
s<t
and
\eta>1
, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N>N_{0}
the following bounds hold:

1.

For all
m,r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]

\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\,\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)=\mathfrak{p}^{c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)},

(138)

where the error term satisfies

\Big|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)\Big|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{m})^{\eta}+CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(139)

For each fixed
m,r\in\mathbb{R}
we have the point-wise convergence

\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)=\mathfrak{p}^{c,c}_{t}(m,r).

(140)

2.

If
s,t\in(-2,2)
and
v+s/2<0
, so that
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
is entirely composed of
v
atoms
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)
for
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, see (
132
), then for each
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, and all
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s),r\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{d,c}_{s,t}\left(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{k}(s),r\right)\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{k}(s),r)}

(141)

where the error term satisfies

\Big|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{k}(s),r)\Big|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(142)

In particular, for each fixed
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s),r\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{d,c}_{s,t}\left(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{k}(s),r\right).

(143)

3.

If
s,t\in(-2,2)
and
u-s/2<0
, so that
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
is entirely composed of
u
atoms
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s)
for
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, see (
132
), then for each
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
and all
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s),r\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{d,c}_{s,t}\left(\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s),r\right)=0.

(144)

Lemma 8.6

The locations and masses of the (finitely many) atoms of the transition probability distributions
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}
and
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}
converge to those of
\mathfrak{p}^{d,d}_{s,t}
and
\pi^{c,d}_{s,t}
. For all
s,t\in[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
with
s<t

1.

If
v+s/2<0
, then for all
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, the discrete support of

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s),\cdot\right)
coincides with the set of points
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)

for
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-t/2\rfloor\rrbracket
(if
v+t/2>0
then there are no atoms) and the masses satisfy the following bound: There exists
C,\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, all
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, and all
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-t/2\rfloor\rrbracket

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s),\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{d,d}_{s,t}\left(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s),\mathbf{v}^{v}_{k}(t)\right)\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}}

(145)

where the error term satisfies

\left|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}\right|\leq CN^{-\chi}.

(146)

In particular,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s),\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{d,d}_{s,t}\left(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s),\mathbf{v}^{v}_{k}(t)\right).

(147)

2.

If
u-s/2<0
, then for all
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, the discrete support of

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s),\cdot\right)
is the set of points
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)
for
k\in\llbracket 0,j\rrbracket
and the masses satisfy the following bound: There exists
C,\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, all
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, and all
k\in\llbracket 0,j\rrbracket

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s),\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{d,d}_{s,t}\left(\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s),\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t)\right)\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}}

(148)

where the error term satisfies

\left|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}\right|\leq CN^{-\chi}.

(149)

In particular,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s),\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{d,d}_{s,t}\left(\mathbf{u}^{u}_{j}(s),\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t)\right).

(150)

3.

If
v+s/2<0
and
m\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}=[0,4N]
, then the measure
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\cdot\right)
has no discrete atomic part. If
u-s/2<0
and
m\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}=[0,4N]
, then the measure
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\cdot\right)
has discrete support equal to the set of points
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)
for
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
and the masses satisfy the following bound:
There exists
C,\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, all
m\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}=[0,4N]
and all
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
,

\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)\right)=\mathfrak{p}^{c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t)\right)\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t)\right)}

(151)

where the error term satisfies

\left|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t)\right)\right|\leq CN^{-\chi}.

(152)

Remark 8.7
.

Lemmas
8.5
and
8.6
prove convergence of the transition probabilities for
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}^{(N)}
to those of
\mathbb{T}
, and Lemmas
8.3
and
8.4
prove the convergence of the their state spaces (in addition to the convergence of a family of marginal distributions). This implies that as Markov processes on the time interval
[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
,
\mathbb{\hat{Y}}^{(N)}_{s}
converges in finite-dimensional distributions to
\mathbb{T}_{s}
. Of course, our results prove precise error bounds on the convergence of the transition probabilities too.

8.4
Proof of Proposition
7.1

Recall
\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
from (
109
).

Lemma 8.8

For
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
,
\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X}),\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{0},\vec{X})\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

We show that
\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X}{\color[rgb]{0,1,0})}\in(0,\infty)
for
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
since
\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{0},\vec{X}{\color[rgb]{0,1,0})}\in(0,\infty)
follows similarly.
For
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
,
s_{k}:=c_{k}+\cdots+c_{d}\in[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,d+1\rrbracket
.
The support of the integrand defining
\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
is
\mathcal{S}_{s_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{S}_{s_{d+1}}
(see Definition
6.2
). For fixed
u
and
v
, there exists a constant
L<0
such that for all
s\in[0,\mathsf{C}_{u,v})
the support
\mathcal{S}_{s}
is lower bounded by
L
. This follows immediately from the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{s}
as the union of
\mathcal{S}^{c}_{s}=(0,\infty)
with
\mathcal{S}^{d}_{s}
, a finite number of negative discrete atoms, whose locations vary continuously with
s
. Owing to this lower bound and the ordering of the
X_{k}
variables, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}

\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\frac{(1-X_{d})}{4}r}\mathfrak{p}_{0}(dr).

(153)

We used
\mathcal{G}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})\leq Ce^{-\frac{(1-X_{d})}{4}r_{d+1}}
for
\vec{r}\in\mathcal{S}_{s_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{S}_{s_{d+1}}
, and then Lemma
6.9
to integrate out the variables
r_{1},\ldots,r_{d}
.

We claim that the integral on the right-hand side of (
153
) is finite.
The atoms in
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
have a finite contribution to the integral on the right-hand side of (
153
), so it remains to control the integral on
(0,\infty)
. In that case, the measure
\mathfrak{p}_{0}(dr)
can be written as
\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{0}(r)dr
where the density function
\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{0}(r)
is given in Definition
6.2

Using the asymptotic behavior of the gamma function for small (
274
) and large (
275
) imaginary parts, we see that for any fixed
u,v
with
u+v>0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

C^{-1}f(r)\leq\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{0}(r)\leq Cf(r)\qquad\mathrm{where}\quad f(r):=\begin{cases}r^{1/2}&r\geq 1\\
r^{u+v-1}&r\in(0,1)\end{cases}.

(154)

Substituting this into the right-hand side of (
153
) and using the integrability of
r^{-1/2}
at 0, and the decay coming from
e^{-\frac{(1-X_{d})}{4}r}
at infinity, we find that the right-hand side of (
153
) is finite.

Turning to the positivity of
\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
for
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
,

\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})\geq\mathcal{G}(\vec{2};\vec{c};\vec{X})\int\limits_{[1,2]^{d+1}}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathfrak{p}^{c,c}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},r_{i})dr_{i}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1})dr_{d+1}.

(155)

The inequality follows from the definition of
\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
and the positivity of the integrand therein in conjunction with the lower bound
\mathcal{G}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})\geq\mathcal{G}(\vec{2};\vec{c};\vec{X})
for
\vec{r}\in[1,2]^{d+1}
and the fact that on
[1,2]
the transition and marginal distributions are absolutely continuous. There exists
C>0
so that
\mathcal{G}(\vec{2};\vec{c};\vec{X})\geq C
for
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
.

It remains to show that the integral in the final line of (
155
) over
[1,2]^{d+1}
is strictly positive for any
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
. Our assumption on
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
implies that
s_{k}-s_{k+1}=c_{k}>0
. Using this and the explicit formulas for the marginal and transition density functions (see Definitions
6.2
and
6.7
), we see that for all
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\mathfrak{p}^{c,c}_{s_{i},s_{i+1}}(r_{i},r_{i+1}),\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1})\geq C
for all
\vec{r}\in[1,2]^{d+1}
.
∎

Turning to the proof of Proposition
7.1
, by Lemma
8.8
we see that in order to prove
\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})\to\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
, it suffices to show that

\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})\to\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
for
\vec{c}\in(0,\mathsf{C}_{d,u,v})^{d}
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
, and for
\vec{c}=\vec{0}
. We will just deal with the first case, since the second case where
\vec{c}=\vec{0}
follows similarly. The idea in the proof is to use the convergence lemmas in Section
8.3
to show point-wise and dominated convergence of the integrand in
\phi^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
to that of
\phi_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
. There is a bit of bookkeeping since the measures we consider have mixed discrete and absolutely continuous support.

In the definition of
\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
we can insert a multiplicative factor
1
as
\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}(\mathbf{1}_{r_{i}\geq 0}+\mathbf{1}_{r_{i}<0})
. Expanding leads to
2^{d+1}
terms, each one corresponding to a choice of whether we integrate over the continuous part of
\hat{\pi}^{(N)}
when
r_{i}\geq 0
or the atomic part when
r_{i}<0
. Explicitly,

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})=\!\!\!\!\sum_{I\subset\llbracket 1,d+1\rrbracket}\sum_{r_{I}}\int_{r_{J}}\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\pi}^{(N),a_{i+1},a_{i}}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},r_{i})\qquad

(156)

\displaystyle\times N^{u+v}\hat{\pi}^{(N),a_{d+1}}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1}).

(157)

Here
J
is the complement of
I
in
\llbracket 1,d+1\rrbracket
; the sum over
r_{I}
is an
|I|
-fold summation over
r_{i}\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s_{i}}
for
i\in I
; the integral over
r_{J}
is really a
|J|
-fold integral as the
r
variables with indices in
J
vary in
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
(note, we have suppressed the
\prod_{j\in J}dr_{j}
symbols); and the variables
a_{i}
take values in the set of symbols
\{c,d\}
with
a_{i}=d
when
i\in I
and
a_{i}=c
when
i\in J
.

As
N
varies, the number of atoms in the atomic parts of
\hat{\pi}^{(N)}
does not change (i.e.
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
is independent of
N
). Therefore, the form of the decomposition (
156
) remains stable with
N
. Moreover,
\tilde{\phi}_{u,v}(\vec{c},\vec{X})
admits the same form of decomposition. Thus, in order to show the convergence of
\tilde{\phi}^{(N)}
to
\tilde{\phi}
, it suffices to show that for any choice of
I
, the corresponding sum over the
r_{I}
and integral in the remaining
r_{J}
variables in (
156
) converges to its proposed limit. The sum over
r_{I}
can be indexed in terms of the labels of the elements chosen from each
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s_{i}}
. By labels we mean that for each
i\in I
, we may identify
r_{i}\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s_{i}}
by a label
\ell_{i}
such that
r_{i}=x^{u}_{\ell_{i}}(s_{i})
or
r_{i}=x^{v}_{\ell_{i}}(s_{i})
(depending on whether we are dealing with
u
or
v
atoms). For each choice of
I
there are a finite number of choices of labels
\{\ell_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. If for every choice of
I
and every choice of labels, we can prove convergence of the remaining integral in the
r_{J}
variables, then we will have achieved our goal of proving the point-wise limit in (
111
).

Lemmas
8.4
and
8.6
show that the locations and masses of the atoms of the
\hat{\pi}^{(N)}
measures converge as
N\to\infty
to those of the
\mathfrak{p}
measures and Lemmas
8.3
and
8.5
show the point-wise convergence of the densities of the absolute continuous parts of the
\hat{\pi}^{(N)}
measures to those of the
\mathfrak{p}
measures. Equation (
124
) in Lemma
8.2
shows how
\mathcal{G}^{(N)}
converges point-wise to
\mathcal{G}
. In light of these results it follows that for each choice of
I
and labels
\{\ell_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, the integrand in (
156
) converges point-wise to its proposed limit. To show convergence of the integral itself, it suffices to demonstrate a dominating function and then use the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem.

We will assume below that
X_{d}<1
. When
X_{d}=1
, the functions
\mathcal{G}^{(N)}
and
\mathcal{G}
do not depend on
r_{d+1}
and thus we can integrate out the
r_{d+1}
variable. Since
\int\hat{\pi}^{(N)}_{s_{d+1},s_{d+1}}(r_{d},dr_{d})\hat{\pi}^{(N)}_{s_{d+1}}(dr_{d+1})=\hat{\pi}^{(N)}_{s_{d}}(dr_{d})
, the formula for
\tilde{\phi}
reduces to a similar one but with one fewer variable, which can be bounded in the same manner as below.

Since
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
has a uniform lower bound as
s
varies, and
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}_{s}=[0,4N]
, we can use (
123
) of Lemma
8.2
to show that there exist
c,C>0
such that

\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})\leq Ce^{-c(r_{1}+\cdots r_{d+1})}

(158)

as the
r_{k}
vary over the
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N)}_{s_{k}}
for
k\in\llbracket 1,d+1\rrbracket
. Using this bound along with Lemmas
8.3
,
8.4
,
8.5
and
8.6
we arrive at the following bound: Fix
\eta=3/2
, then there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
c,c^{\prime},C,C^{\prime},\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N>N_{0}
, all
r_{i}\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s_{i}}
with
i\in I
and all
r_{j}\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
for
j\in J
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})\prod_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\pi}^{(N),a_{i+1},a_{i}}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},r_{i})\cdot N^{u+v}\hat{\pi}^{(N),a_{d+1}}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1})\right|

(159)

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ce^{-c(r_{1}+\cdots r_{d+1})}\prod_{j\in J}e^{CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r_{j}})^{\eta}}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{i+1},a_{i}}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},r_{i})\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{a_{d+1}}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1})

(160)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}(r_{1}+\cdots r_{d+1})}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{i+1},a_{i}}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},r_{i})\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{a_{d+1}}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1})

For the first inequality we used (
158
) along with the bounds from Lemmas
8.3
and
8.5
; the second inequality uses
e^{-cr}e^{CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}}\leq C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}r}
for a large enough
C^{\prime}>0
and a small enough
c^{\prime}>0
.

The point of (
159
) is that it now provides us with an
N
-independent dominating function. If we can show that for each
I\subset\llbracket 1,d+1\rrbracket
,

\sum_{r_{I}}\int_{r_{J}}C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}(r_{1}+\cdots r_{d+1})}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{i+1},a_{i}}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},r_{i})\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{a_{d+1}}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1})<\infty

(161)

we will be done owing to the point-wise convergence we have already shown.

Let us first consider (
161
) with
I
such that
d+1\in I
. In that case, the
r_{d+1}
variable is summed over the finite number of atoms in
\mathcal{S}^{d}_{0}
, each of which has a finite mass. Thus, for such terms in (
161
) we can bound
\mathfrak{p}^{a_{I(d+1)}}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1})\leq C
. All of the other terms
\mathfrak{p}^{a_{i+1},a_{i}}_{s_{i+1},s_{i}}(r_{i+1},r_{i})
are either densities or masses of probability measures. Owing to this and the fact that
e^{-c^{\prime}r}
is upper bounded by a constant for
r\in\mathcal{S}_{s}
it immediately follows that the sum over
r_{I}
and integral over
r_{J}
is likewise bounded by a constant.

For
I
such that
d+1\notin I
, the
r_{d+1}
variable is integrated over
\mathcal{S}^{c}=(0,\infty)
. The term
\mathfrak{p}^{a_{d+1}}_{s_{d+1}}(r_{d+1})
now represents the density of that infinite measure. As in the previous paragraph we may integrate/sum out all of the other variables
r_{1},\ldots,r_{d}
at the cost of a constant factor. Thus, we are left to bound

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-c^{\prime}r}\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{0}(r)dr<\infty
, which is done precisely as in the proof of Lemma
8.8
.
This shows that the right-hand side of (
159
) is a dominating function, completing the proof of Proposition
7.1
.

8.5
Proof of lemmas in Section
8.3

8.5.1
Proof of Lemma
8.2

To prove (
123
) we show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})

\displaystyle={\bf 1}_{\vec{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\leq 4N}}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}\left(1+\sinh^{2}\left(\tfrac{s_{k}}{2\sqrt{N}}\right)-\tfrac{r_{k}}{4N}\right)^{N(X^{(N)}_{k}-X^{(N)}_{k-1})}

(162)

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}\exp\left(\tfrac{N(X^{(N)}_{k}-X^{(N)}_{k-1})}{4N}\left(4N\sinh^{2}\left(\tfrac{s_{k}}{2\sqrt{N}}\right)-r_{k}\right)\right)

(163)

\displaystyle\leq C\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}e^{-(X^{(N)}_{k}-X^{(N)}_{k-1})\frac{r_{k}}{4}}\leq C\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}e^{-(X_{k}-X_{k-1})\frac{r_{k}}{4}+\frac{|r_{k}|}{2N}}.

(164)

The first equality is by the definition of
\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})
and the hyperbolic trigonometric identity

\frac{\cosh(x)+1}{2}=1+\sinh^{2}(\tfrac{x}{2})
. The next inequality
uses that
(1+x)^{a}\leq e^{ax}
for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq-1}
. In particular, we take
a=N(X^{(N)}_{k}-X^{(N)}_{k-1})

and
x=\sinh^{2}\left(\tfrac{s_{k}}{2\sqrt{N}}\right)-\tfrac{r_{k}}{4N}
. The
\sinh^{2}
is always non-negative
and due to the indicator function
{\bf 1}_{\vec{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{\leq 4N}}
we may assume that
\tfrac{r_{k}}{4N}\leq 1
. After applying
the inequality we drop the indicator function. The next inequality
relies on the fact that
N\sinh^{2}\left(\tfrac{s_{k}}{2\sqrt{N}}\right)

can be bounded above by a constant provided that the
s_{k}
vary in a
compact set (which follows from the assumption on the
\vec{c}\in I^{d}
). The constant
C
will depend on the set
I
. The final
inequality uses the fact that
|X^{(N)}_{k}-X_{k}|\leq N^{-1},
which means that we can replace the
X^{(N)}_{k}
by their limiting values
X_{k}
at the cost of introducing the factor
|r_{k}|/2N
in the exponential.

Owing to the triangle inequality, to prove (
124
) it suffices to show that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})=\mathcal{G}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X}),\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left|\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r}^{(N)};\vec{c};\vec{X})-\mathcal{G}^{(N)}(\vec{r};\vec{c};\vec{X})\right|=0.

(165)

The first limit in (
165
) follows immediately from Taylor expansion of the
\sinh
function and the convergence of
(1+x/N)^{N}
to
e^{x}
and
X^{(N)}_{k}
to
X_{k}
.

The second limit in (
165
) will make use of two elementary inequalities. The first is that for all
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d+1},b_{1},\ldots,b_{d+1}\in\mathbb{R}
bounded in absolute value by
M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

\left|\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}a_{k}-\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}b_{k}\right|\leq M^{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d+1}|a_{k}-b_{k}|.

We apply this inequality with

a_{k}^{(N)}=\Big(1+\sinh^{2}\left(\tfrac{s_{k}}{2\sqrt{N}}\right)-\tfrac{r^{(N)}_{k}}{4N}\Big)^{N(X^{(N)}_{k}-X^{(N)}_{k-1})}

and

b_{k}^{(N)}=\Big(1+\sinh^{2}\left(\tfrac{s_{k}}{2\sqrt{N}}\right)-\tfrac{r_{k}}{4N}\Big)^{N(X^{(N)}_{k}-X^{(N)}_{k-1})}.

For
\vec{X},\vec{s}
fixed, it is easy to see that we can find some
M
large enough so that
|a^{(N)}_{k}|,|b^{(N)}_{k}|\leq M
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,d+1\rrbracket
and all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Thus, it suffices to show that
\lim_{N\to\infty}|a^{(N)}_{k}-b^{(N)}_{k}|=0
. Notice that
a^{(N)}_{k}
can be written in the form
(1+\tilde{a}^{(N)}_{k}/L)^{L}
where
L=N(X_{k}-X_{k-1})
. There exists some compact interval
I
such that
\tilde{a}^{(N)}_{k}\in I
for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Likewise
b^{(N)}_{k}
can be written in the same form in terms of
\tilde{b}^{(N)}_{k}
and we can find some compact interval
I
so that
\tilde{b}^{(N)}_{k}\in I
for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
as well. The convergence
\lim_{N\to\infty}|r^{(N)}-r|=0
implies that
\lim_{N\to\infty}|\tilde{a}^{(N)}_{k}-\tilde{b}^{(N)}_{k}|=0
. To finish the proof, we use the following elementary inequality: For any compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
L
large enough and
\tilde{a},\tilde{b}\in I
,
|(1+\tilde{a}/L)^{L}-(1+\tilde{b}/L)^{L}|\leq C|\tilde{a}-\tilde{b}|
. This implies the second limit in (
165
) and completes the proof.

8.5.2
Notation for asymptotics

Recall
{\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}[\kappa;z]
from (
37
) and (
38
). For
k,N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
, define

{\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}_{N}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{k}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}[\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{N}},z_{i}].

(166)

Here we have fixed that
\kappa=\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{N}}
, in which case
q=e^{-\kappa}
.

Setting
m=1
in Proposition
2.3
shows that for
q=e^{-\kappa}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

\log(\pm q^{z};q)_{\infty}={\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}[\kappa;z]+{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{1}[\kappa;z]

(167)

where
{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{1}[\kappa;z]
satisfies (
41
).
For
k,N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
, define

{\mathsf{E}}^{\pm}_{N}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{k}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{1}[\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{N}},z_{i}].

(168)

8.5.3
Proof of Lemma
8.3

For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\lim_{N\to\infty}\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)=0
, so (
127
) follows from (
126
).
It remains to prove (
126
).
We proceed in three steps. In step 1, we write down
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)
. In step 2, we further rewrite
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)
in terms of a limiting term and error terms as in Section
8.5.2
. In step 3, we control the error terms using the bounds in Proposition
2.3
.

Step 1.
For
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
, in light of (
61
), (
68
) and (
115
) we have

\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)=\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t;v,1,u,1}(r)

(169)

where we define

\displaystyle\qquad\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t;v,\tilde{v},u,\tilde{u}}(r):=\frac{1}{8\pi N\sqrt{\frac{r}{4N}}\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}}\times

(170)

\displaystyle\dfrac{\big(q,-q^{v+\tilde{v}+t},q^{v+u},-q^{v+\tilde{u}},-q^{u+\tilde{v}},q^{\tilde{v}+\tilde{u}},-q^{u+\tilde{u}-t}\big)_{\infty}\Big\lvert\big(q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}}\big)_{\infty}\Big\rvert^{2}}{\big(q^{v+\tilde{v}+u+\tilde{u}}\big)_{\infty}\Big\lvert\big(q^{v+t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},-q^{\tilde{v}+t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},q^{u-t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},-q^{\tilde{u}-t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},\big)_{\infty}\Big\rvert^{2}}

(171)

and where we have used the notation (suppressing the
N
dependence above)

\theta_{r}=\theta^{(N)}_{r}:=\arccos\left(1-\frac{r}{2N}\right).

(172)

Observe that if we send
r\mapsto 4N-r
, then
\theta_{r}\mapsto\pi-\theta_{r}
and thus also
q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}}\mapsto-q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}}
and
q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}}\mapsto q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}}
. These transformations imply that
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t;v,\tilde{v},u,\tilde{u}}(r)
transforms under this change of variables by swapping the tilde and non-tilde variables:

\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t;v,\tilde{v},u,\tilde{u}}(4N-r)=\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t;\tilde{v},v,\tilde{u},u}(r).

(173)

In light of this transformation we will now consider the asymptotics behavior of
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t;v,\tilde{v},u,\tilde{u}}(r)
for
r\in[0,2N]
. We will show (generalizing (
125
)) that, provided
u+v>0
and
\tilde{u}+\tilde{v}>0
,
r\in[0,2N]

N^{u+v}\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\,\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t;v,\tilde{v},u,\tilde{u}}=\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t;v,u}(r)\,\cdot\,e^{\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t;v,\tilde{v},u,\tilde{u}}(r)}

(174)

where the error term satisfies the bound

\Big|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t;v,\tilde{v},u,\tilde{u}}(r)\Big|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(175)

as in (
126
) and where
\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t;v,u}=\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}
is given in Definition
6.2
(and does not depend on
\tilde{v}
or
\tilde{u}
).
By combining this bound, the transformation (
173
) and the growth bound (
154
) on
\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}
we can easily deduce that equation (
125
) also holds for
r\in[2N,4N]
with the claimed error bound (
126
). Thus, we focus the rest of this proof on demonstrating (
175
) under the restriction
r\in[0,2N]
.

Step 2.

By Taylor expanding around
r=0
we can write

\sqrt{N}\theta^{(N)}_{r}=\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r),

(176)

where
{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)
is the remainder.
With this and (
167
), we rewrite (
170
) as

\displaystyle\qquad\log\left[\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\,\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t;v,\tilde{v},u,\tilde{u}}(r)\right]=-\log\left[8\pi N\sqrt{\dfrac{r}{4N}}\,\right]+{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r})+{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)+{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r).

(177)

In the above formula, for
a\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have defined

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(a):=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{N}\Big[1,v+u,\tilde{v}+\tilde{u},{i}a,-{i}a\Big]+{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{N}\Big[v+\tilde{v}+t,v+\tilde{u},u+\tilde{v},u+\tilde{u}-t\Big]

(178)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{N}\Big[v+u+\tilde{v}+\tilde{u},v+\dfrac{t}{2}+{i}\dfrac{a}{2},v+\dfrac{t}{2}-{i}\dfrac{a}{2},u-\dfrac{t}{2}+{i}\dfrac{a}{2},u-\dfrac{t}{2}-{i}\dfrac{a}{2}\Big]

(179)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{N}\Big[\tilde{v}+\dfrac{t}{2}+{i}\dfrac{a}{2},\tilde{v}+\dfrac{t}{2}-{i}\dfrac{a}{2},\tilde{u}-\dfrac{t}{2}+{i}\dfrac{a}{2},\tilde{u}-\dfrac{t}{2}-{i}\dfrac{a}{2}\Big].

(180)

Here
{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)
comes from the Taylor expansion (
176
) and is given by

{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r):={\mathcal{A}}_{N}\left(\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\right)-{\mathcal{A}}_{N}\big(\sqrt{r}\big).

(181)

Define the function
\mathcal{E}_{N}(a)
exactly as in (
180
), except with the
{\mathcal{A}}
symbol replaced by
{\mathsf{E}}_{N}
, as in (
168
).
{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)
comes from Proposition
2.3
and is

{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)=\mathcal{E}_{N}\left(\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\right)=\mathcal{E}_{N}\left(\sqrt{N}\theta^{(N)}_{r}\right).

(182)

The first two terms on the right-hand side of (
177
) can be simplified
considerably using the explicit expressions for
{\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}[\kappa;z]
from (
37
) and (
38
). In particular, when
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r})
is expanded, all of the terms that have a prefactor
\pi^{2}
coming from (
37
) and (
38
) end up canceling out. Combining the remaining terms we find that

\displaystyle-\log\left[8\pi N\sqrt{\dfrac{r}{4N}}\,\right]+{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r})=

(183)

\displaystyle\log\left[\frac{\Gamma(v+u+\tilde{v}+\tilde{u})}{\Gamma(v+u)\Gamma(\tilde{v}+\tilde{u})}\frac{\left|\Gamma\left(u-\frac{t}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2},v+\frac{t}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2}\right)\right|^{2}}{8\pi\sqrt{r}\,\cdot\,\left|\Gamma\left({i}\sqrt{r}\right)\right|^{2}}\right]-(v+u)\log(N).

(184)

Recalling (
169
), it follows by taking
\tilde{v}=\tilde{u}=1
in the above formula and using
\tfrac{\Gamma(v+u+2)}{\Gamma(v+u)}=(v+u)(v+u+1)
that (recall also the formula in Definition
6.2
for
\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}
)

\log\left[N^{u+v}\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\,\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)\right]=\log\left[\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}(r)\right]+{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)+{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r).

(185)

Similarly, using (
173
) we see that

\log\left[N^{2}\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\,\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(4N-r)\right]=\log\left[\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}(r)\right]+{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)+{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)

(186)

where
{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)
are defined exactly as
{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)
are above but with
(v,u)
and
(\tilde{v},\tilde{u})
