swapped.

Step 3.

It remains to bound for
r\in[0,2N]
the two error terms
{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)
in (
185
) and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)
in (
186
). The analysis is exactly the same in both cases so we will just focus on the first set of error terms. To be precise, in this step we will show that for all
\eta>1
, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi>0
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
r\in[0,2N]

\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)\right|,\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)\right|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(187)

From this and (
185
), the desired bound in (
126
) immediately follows. Thus we will show (
187
). In demonstrating those bounds we will also need to control
{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)
, so we start with that.

Bounding
|{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)|
.

For
r\in[0,2N]
,

\sqrt{N}\theta^{(N)}_{r}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}r}\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\geq 0.

(188)

The first inequality in (
188
) is equivalent to the inequality

\arccos(1-x/2)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}x}\qquad\textrm{ for }x\in[0,2]

(189)

which can easily be shown by matching the values at
x=0
and
x=2
of both sides and then showing that the derivative of the difference strictly decreases (hence the difference is strictly concave). The second inequality in (
188
) is equivalent to
\arccos(1-x/2)-\sqrt{x}\geq 0
for
x\in[0,2]
and is also shown by taking derivatives.

Now we claim that for all
\eta>1
there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi>0
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,2N]
,

{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\log(N)\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(190)

Note that by (
188
),
{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\geq 0
. Changing variables
x=r/N
, (
190
) reduces this to

\arccos\left(1-\frac{x}{2}\right)-\sqrt{x}\leq\frac{C(1+\sqrt{Nx})^{\eta}}{N^{\chi+1/2}\log(N)}\qquad\mathrm{for}\quad x\in[0,2].

(191)

We split the demonstration of (
191
) into two cases.
Let
x_{N}
be such that
\tfrac{(1+\sqrt{Nx_{N}})^{\eta-1}}{N^{\chi}\log(N)}=1
. Then, since
\tfrac{(1+\sqrt{Nx})^{\eta-1}}{N^{\chi}\log(N)}\geq 1
for
x\in[x_{N},2]
, we have on that interval that

\arccos\left(1-\frac{x}{2}\right)-\sqrt{x}\leq C\sqrt{x}\leq C\frac{1+\sqrt{Nx}}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{(1+\sqrt{Nx})^{\eta-1}}{N^{\chi}\log(N)}\leq\frac{C(1+\sqrt{Nx})^{\eta}}{N^{\chi+1/2}\log(N)}

(192)

where in the first inequality we can take
C=\sqrt{\pi^{2}/8}-1
owing to (
189
). We do not require anything on the value of
\chi
. This shows (
191
) for
x\in[x_{N},2]
.

Tuning to the case of
x\in[0,x_{N}]
, observe that
x_{N}
goes to zero as
N
grows and
x_{N}\leq N^{\frac{2\chi}{\eta-1}-1}\log(N)^{\frac{2}{\eta-1}}
. Provided that
\chi<\tfrac{\eta-1}{2}
(so
x_{N}
goes to zero as a power law in
N
), for
x\in[0,x_{N}]
we can use Taylor expansion with remainder to show that there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
x\in[0,x_{N}]
,

\arccos\left(1-\frac{x}{2}\right)-\sqrt{x}\leq Cx^{3/2}.

From this we see that all that is left is to show that on the interval
x\in[0,x_{N}]
,

x^{3/2}\leq C\frac{(1+\sqrt{Nx})^{\eta}}{N^{\chi+1/2}\log(N)}
.
Clearly this is true when
x=0
(where both sides are zero). If we can show that the derivatives are likewise ordered on
x\in[0,x_{N}]
then the above inequality will immediately follow. Calculating those derivatives we find that showing their ordering reduces to showing that

x\leq\frac{(1+\sqrt{Nx})^{\eta-1}}{N^{\chi}\log(N)}

which easily follows from Taylor expansion with remainder on
(1+\sqrt{Nx})^{\eta-1}
. Thus, we have shown (
190
).

Bounding
|{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)|
.
Recall that
\mathcal{E}_{N}(r)
is defined in (
182
) (the function
\mathcal{E}_{N}(a)
is defined exactly as in (
180
), except with the
{\mathcal{A}}
symbol replaced by
{\mathsf{E}}_{N}
, as in (
168
)) as a sum of many terms of the form
{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{1}[\kappa,z]
for
\kappa=\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{N}}
and for various choices of the variable
z
. Some of the assignments of the variable
z
depend on
r
while others do not (though may still depend on other variables like
u,v,t
). We refer to the former terms as Type (1) and the later as Type (2). For Type (2) terms, we see from (
41
) in Proposition
2.3
that for any
z
fixed and
b\in(0,1)
, we can find a
\kappa_{0}>0
and a
C>0
so that for all
\kappa<\kappa_{0}
(or equivalently we can find
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
so that for all
N>N_{0}
),

|{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{1}[\kappa,z]|\leq C(\kappa+\kappa^{b})\leq C^{\prime}N^{-b/2}.

(193)

From this we see that the contribution of Type (2) terms to
|{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)|
satisfies the bound in (
187
).

Now, let us consider how Type (1) terms contributes to
|{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)|
. These terms take the form
{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{1}[\kappa,z]
for either
z=z_{c}(r)=c\pm{i}\sqrt{N}\tfrac{\theta^{(N)}_{r}}{2}
with some fixed real
c
(e.g.
c=0
or
c=u-\tfrac{t}{2}
) or
z=\pm{i}\sqrt{N}\theta^{(N)}_{r}
. Call the first type of term Type (1a) and the second Type (1b). Observe that for
r\in[0,2N]
,
\theta^{(N)}_{r}\in[0,\pi/2]
. This means that
\sqrt{N}\theta^{(N)}_{r}\in[0,\frac{\pi}{\kappa}]
. For Type (1a) terms this range is further divided by
2
and hence
|\textup{Im}(z)|\leq\frac{\alpha}{\kappa}
for
\alpha=\pi/2
. Since this
\alpha<\pi
, we can apply (
41
) from Proposition
2.3
to show that for any
b\in(0,1)
and
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/2)
, there exists
C,\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa<\kappa_{0}
and
r\in[0,2N]

\Big|{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{1}[\kappa,z]\Big|\leq C\left(\kappa(1+|z|)^{2}+\kappa^{b}(1+|z|)^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}\right)

(194)

where
z=c\pm{i}\sqrt{N}\tfrac{\theta^{(N)}_{r}}{2}
. For the Type (1b) terms, observe that it is only
{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{1}
that arises in
{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)
. Recalling from Proposition
2.3
that the bound (
41
) on
{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}[\kappa,z]
holds with
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{2\alpha}{\kappa}
(for
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
) we see that (
194
) holds when
\pm
is restricted to
+
and
z=c\pm{i}\sqrt{N}\theta^{(N)}_{r}
.

It just remains to massage the bound in (
194
) into the claimed form. To do this, note that for Type (1) choices of
z=z(r)
, with
r\in[0,2N]
, we have that
|z(r)|\leq c+C\sqrt{r}
for some choices of
c,C>0
. This implies that for any
b\in(0,1)
and
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/2)
, there exists
C>0
such that for all
r\in[0,2N]
,

\Big|{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{1}[\kappa,z_{c}(r)]\Big|\leq C\left(N^{-1/2}(1+\sqrt{r})^{2}+N^{-b/2}(1+\sqrt{r})^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}\right).

(195)

The second term on the right-hand side above is already of the form
CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
where
\eta
can be taken arbitrarily close to
1
by turning
b
and
{\varepsilon}
close to zero, and where
\chi=b/2
. The first term
N^{-1/2}(1+\sqrt{r})^{2}
can also be put in this form since for a large enough constant
C>0
,

N^{-1/2}(1+\sqrt{r})^{2}=N^{-1/2}(1+\sqrt{r})^{2-\eta}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}\leq CN^{-\frac{\eta-1}{2}}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}

(196)

where the inequality uses that
1+\sqrt{r}\leq C^{\prime}N^{1/2}
for some suitably large constant
C^{\prime}>0
. Taking
\chi=\tfrac{\eta-1}{2}
puts this bound in the form
CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
. Therefore, we conclude that the contribution of Type (1) terms to
|{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)|
satisfies the bound in (
187
). Combining this with the previous conclusion for Type (2) terms, we arrive at the bound in (
187
) on
|{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r)|
.

Bounding
|{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)|
.
Recall that
{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)
is defined in (
181
) in terms of
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(a)
defined in (
180
). From (
180
), we see that
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r))-{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r})
involves many cancellations. All of the terms in (
180
) which do not depend on the argument
a
immediately cancel when taking this difference. Recalling the definition of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}[\kappa,z]
from (
37
) and (
38
), we also see that the terms in those functions involving
\pi^{2}
do not depend on the
z
argument and hence also cancel upon taking a difference. Let us take an accounting of which terms remain. From
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
, we need to account for (1) the
(z-\tfrac{1}{2})\log(\kappa)
terms and (2) the
\log\left[\tfrac{\Gamma(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]
terms, while from
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}
, we need only account for (3) the
(z-\tfrac{1}{2})\log(2)
terms. Let us consider each of these types of term separately and show how their contributions can be bounded by expressions of the form of the right-hand side of (
187
).

Type (1) terms contribute to
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r))-{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r})
expressions of the form
{i}\frac{{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{2}\log(\kappa)
. Since
\kappa=\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{N}}
, the magnitude of such terms is proportional to
{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\log(N)
.
The bound we established in (
190
) implies that the contribution to
|{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)|
of Type (1) terms can be bounded by
CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
provided
\chi
is small enough. This is precisely of the form of the right-hand side of (
187
). Note that Type (3) terms which arise from
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}
involve
{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\log(2)
. Since for
N\geq 2
,
\log(2)\leq\log(N)
, the argument above immediately controls those terms by
CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
as well.

All that remains is to control the contribution to
|{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)|
from the Type (2) terms coming from
\log\left[\tfrac{\Gamma(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]
in
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
. These contributions are of the form

\log\left[\Gamma(z^{\prime}_{c}(r))\right]-\log\left[\Gamma(z_{c}(r))\right]

where
z^{\prime}_{c}(r)=c\pm{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{2}

and
z_{c}(r)=c\pm{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2}
(case (1)), or
z^{\prime}_{c}(r)=c\pm{i}\left(\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\right)

and
z_{c}(r)=c\pm{i}\sqrt{r}
(case (2)). The proof in both cases is
identical (just the constants change), so we focus on the first case. Similarly, the argument we
present works just as well for
\pm=+
and
\pm=-
, so we will just
address the
+
case. We claim that for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
fixed, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi>0
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]

\Big|\log[\Gamma(z^{\prime}_{c}(r))]-\log[\Gamma(z_{c}(r))]\Big|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(197)

We will show this separately for
r\in[r_{0},4N]
and for
r\in[0,r_{0}]
, where
r_{0}
is specified momentarily. The purpose of this split is that for large
r
we can use the asymptotic behavior of the Gamma function in the imaginary direction. For small
r
we can use the uniform continuity of the gamma function provided
c\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. If
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, we need to account for the divergence from the pole, but after doing that we can still show the desired bound. We proceed with this argument now.

Observe that by the fundamental theorem of calculus, for
r>0
,

\log[\Gamma(z^{\prime}_{c}(r))]-\log[\Gamma(z_{c}(r))]=\int^{\frac{\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{2}}_{\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2}}\!\!\!\!\partial_{y}\log[\Gamma(c+{i}y)]dy=\int^{\frac{\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{2}}_{\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2}}\!\!\!\!{i}\psi(c+{i}y)dy

(198)

where in the second equality uses the Cauchy-Riemann equation to write
\partial_{y}\log[\Gamma(c+{i}y)]={i}\psi(c+{i}y)
where
\psi
is the digamma function. Thus,

\Big|\log[\Gamma(z^{\prime}_{c}(r))]-\log[\Gamma(z_{c}(r))]\Big|\leq\tfrac{{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{2}\!\!\!\!\sup_{y\in\big[\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2},\frac{\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{2}\big]}|\psi(c+{i}y)|.

(199)

It follows from the asymptotic expansion for the digamma function in
[
26
, equation (5.11.2)]
or
[
40
, page 18]
that any
c
there exists
C,y_{0}>0
such that for
|y|>y_{0}
,
|\psi(c+{i}y)|\leq C\log(|y|)
. Let
r_{0}=4y_{0}^{2}
(so that
\tfrac{\sqrt{r_{0}}}{2}=y_{0}
). Thus there exists
C,C^{\prime}>0
such that for
r>r_{0}
,

\Big|\log[\Gamma(z^{\prime}_{c}(r))]-\log[\Gamma(z_{c}(r))]\Big|\leq C{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\log[|z^{\prime}_{c}(r)|]\leq C^{\prime}{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\log(N)

(200)

where the second inequality comes from the fact that for
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
we can bound
|z^{\prime}_{c}(r)|\leq C^{\prime\prime}\log(N)
for some
C^{\prime\prime}>0
. Now we may appeal to (
190
) to bound the right-hand side above by
CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
as desired in (
197
).

It remains to demonstrate (
197
) when
r\in[0,r_{0}]
. First assume that
c\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. For
z
in any compact set away from
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, analyticity implies that
|\psi(z)|
is uniformly continuous. Combining this observation with (
199
), we find that
\big|\log[\Gamma(z^{\prime}_{c}(r))]-\log[\Gamma(z_{c}(r))]\big|\leq C{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)
. Again, owing to (
190
) we may bound this above by
CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
as desired in (
197
).

For the case when
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
we must appeal to the behavior of
\psi(z)
near its poles
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. As in
[
40
, page 14]
, we have that for
z
in a vertical strip with real part in
[c-1/2,c+1/2]
, the function
\psi(z)=-(z-c)^{-1}+\psi_{c}(z)
where
\psi_{c}(z)
is analytic in the strip. This and (
198
) imply

\log(\Gamma(z^{\prime}_{c}(r)))-\log(\Gamma(z_{c}(r)))=\int^{\frac{\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{2}}_{\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2}}\!\!\!\!\left(-y^{-1}+\psi_{c}(c+{i}y)\right)dy.

(201)

This shows that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\Big|\log(\Gamma(z^{\prime}_{c}(r)))-\log(\Gamma(z_{c}(r)))\Big|\leq C\log\left(\tfrac{\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{\sqrt{r}}\right)+C{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r).

(202)

The second term
{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)|
is bounded by appealing to (
190
). For the first,

\log\left(\tfrac{\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{\sqrt{r}}\right)=\log\left(1+\tfrac{{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{\sqrt{r}}\right)\leq\tfrac{{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)}{\sqrt{r}}\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\tfrac{r}{N},

(203)

for some constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
.
Since
r\in[0,r_{0}]
, for any choice of
\eta>1
and
\chi<1
there is a constant
C>0
such that
\tfrac{r}{N}\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
. This bound is of the form of (
197
). Putting together the cases considered above we have shown that (
197
) holds. This completes the proof of (
187
) for
|{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}_{N}(r)|
and hence completes Step 3 and the proof of Lemma
8.3
.

8.5.4
Proof of Lemma
8.4

The convergence of the location of the atoms in (
133
) and (
134
) follows immediately from (
132
) by Taylor expanding in
N
.
The convergence of the masses of these atoms in (
137
) follows immediately from the bounds in (
136
). Thus, our problem reduces to showing (
136
). We will deal with the case when
v+t/2<0
since the other case of
u-t/2<0
proceeds exactly in the same manner. From (
64
) we can write down the weights of the atoms as (recall the parametrization for
a,b,c,d
from (
129
))

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{0}(t))

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\left(q^{-2v-t},-q^{u+1-t},-q^{1+u},q^{2}\right)_{\infty}}{\left(-q^{1-v},q^{u-v-t},-q^{1-v-t},q^{2+v+u}\right)_{\infty}},

(204)

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(t))}{\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{0}(t))}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\left(q^{2v+t},-q^{v+1+t},q^{v+u},-q^{v+1}\right)_{j}\cdot\left(1-q^{2v+2j+t}\right)}{\left(q,-q^{v},q^{1-u+v+t},-q^{v+t}\right)_{j}\cdot\left(1-q^{2v+t}\right)}\cdot\left(q^{-v-u-1}\right)^{j},

(205)

where
j\in\llbracket 1,-v-t/2\rrbracket
. Since
v+t/2<0
and
u+v>0
, there is no chance of division by zero in the above formulas.

Recalling the notation from (
166
) and (
168
), Proposition

2.3
with
m=1
yields (here
({\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}_{N}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\pm}_{N})[\cdots]:={\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}_{N}[\cdots]+{\mathsf{E}}^{\pm}_{N}[\cdots]
)

\displaystyle\log\left(N^{u+v}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{0}(t))\right)=(u+v)\log N+({\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{N}+{\mathsf{E}}^{+}_{N})[-2v-t,2]

(206)

\displaystyle-({\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{N}+{\mathsf{E}}^{+}_{N})[u-v-t,2+v+u]+({\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{N}+{\mathsf{E}}^{-}_{N})[u+1-t,u+1]

(207)

\displaystyle-({\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{N}+{\mathsf{E}}^{-}_{N})[1-v,1-v-t].

(208)

Simplifying the
{\mathcal{A}}
terms, the
N
dependence drops and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{N}[-2v-t,2]-{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{N}[u-v-t,2+v+u]+{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{N}[u+1-t,u+1]

(209)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{N}[1-v,1-v-t]+(u+v)\log N=\log\left[\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{d}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(t))\right],

(210)

where
\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{d}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(t))
is given in Definition
6.2
.

Since
u,v
and
t
are fixed, it follows from (
41
) that the four error terms in (
208
) can be bounded in absolute value by
CN^{-\chi}
for some
C>0
and any
\chi\in(0,1/2)
(recall that
\kappa=2N^{-1/2}
).
Combining this observation with (
210
) proves (
136
) for
j=0
.

In the same manner, we can bound the asymptotic behavior of the other
j\in\llbracket 1,-v-t/2\rrbracket
masses. In order to do this we must take into account the additional multiplicative terms, all of which are of the form
1-q^{a}
,
1+q^{a}
, or
q^{a}
for some choices of real
a
. We use the following error bound:
For
a\in\mathbb{R}
, if we write

\frac{1-q^{a}}{1-q}=a\,\cdot\,e^{{\mathsf{E}}^{1}_{N}(a)},\qquad\frac{1+q^{a}}{1+q}=e^{{\mathsf{E}}^{2}_{N}(a)},\qquad q^{a}=e^{{\mathsf{E}}^{2}_{N}(a)}

(211)

then there exist constants
C_{a}>0
such that

\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{1}_{N}(a)\right|,\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{2}_{N}(a)\right|,\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{3}_{N}(a)\right|\leq C_{a}N^{-1/2}.

(212)

Applying these bounds to the formula for
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(t))
in (
204
) and rewriting the additional factors multiplying
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(t))
in the form of
1-q^{a}
,
1+q^{a}
and
q^{a}
for various choices of
a
, we see that

\displaystyle\dfrac{\left(q^{2v+t},-q^{v+1+t},q^{v+u},-q^{v+1}\right)_{j}\left(1-q^{2v+2j+t}\right)}{\left(q,-q^{v},q^{1-u+v+t},-q^{v+t}\right)_{j}\left(1-q^{2v+t}\right)}\left(q^{-v-u-1}\right)^{j}

(213)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(v+j+t/2)\cdot\left[2v+t,v+u\right]_{j}}{(v+t/2)\cdot j!\left[1-u+v+t\right]_{j}}e^{{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{M_{j}}}

(214)

where

\left|{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{M_{j}}\right|\leq CN^{-1/2}

for some constant
C>0
.
Recognizing that

\dfrac{\Gamma(u-v-t,2+v+u)}{\Gamma(-2v-t)}\cdot\dfrac{(v+j+t/2)\cdot\left[2v+t,v+u\right]_{j}}{(v+t/2)\cdot j!\left[1-u+v+t\right]_{j}}=\mathfrak{p}_{t}^{d}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(t)),

(215)

we arrive at (
136
), thus completing the proof of Lemma
8.4
.

8.5.5
Proof of Lemma
8.5

There are three parts to this lemma.

Part 1.

Observe that (
140
) immediately follows from (
139
). It remains to prove the bound (
139
). The proof of this result very closely follows that of Lemma
8.3
.
From (
116
) it follows that for
m,r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
,

\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)=\dfrac{1}{2N}\pi^{(N),c,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right)\cdot\dfrac{\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)}{\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)}.

(216)

Lemma
8.3
controls
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)
and

\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)
. Thus, we need only to control

\pi^{(N),c,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right).

It is useful to factorize this in order to utilize certain symmetries. Define

\displaystyle f_{t;u,\tilde{u}}^{(N),c}(r):=\bigg\lvert\left(-q^{\tilde{u}+t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}/2},q^{u+t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}/2}\right)_{\infty}\bigg\rvert^{2},

(217)

\displaystyle g_{s;u,\tilde{u}}^{(N),c}(m):=\frac{\bigg\lvert\left(q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{m}};q\right)_{\infty}\bigg\rvert^{2}}{\bigg\lvert\left(-q^{\tilde{u}+s/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{m}/2},q^{u+s/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{m}/2}\right)_{\infty}\bigg\rvert^{2}},

(218)

\displaystyle h_{s,t;u,\tilde{u}}^{(N),c}(m,r):=\frac{1}{4\pi N\sqrt{\frac{m}{N}}\sqrt{1-\frac{m}{4N}}}

(219)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\left(q,-q^{u+\tilde{u}+s},q^{t-s}\right)_{\infty}}{\left(-q^{u+\tilde{u}+t}\right)_{\infty}}\frac{1}{\bigg\lvert\left(q^{(t-s)/2+{i}\sqrt{N}(\theta_{r}+\theta_{m})/2},q^{(t-s)/2+{i}\sqrt{N}(-\theta_{r}+\theta_{m})/2}\right)_{\infty}\bigg\rvert^{2}},

(220)

where we used the notation from (
172
) (suppressing the
N
dependence above) that

\theta_{a}=\theta^{(N)}_{a}:=\arccos\left(1-\frac{a}{2N}\right).

Defining

\displaystyle\pi^{(N),c,c}_{q^{t},q^{s},u,\tilde{u}}\left(x,y\right):=

(222)

\displaystyle 2Nf_{t;u,\tilde{u}}^{(N),c}\left(2N(1-x)\right)\cdot g_{s;u,\tilde{u}}^{(N),c}\left(2N(1-y)\right)\cdot h_{s,t;u,\tilde{u}}^{(N),c}\left(2N(1-x),2N(1-y)\right)

(223)

it follows from (
61
) that

\displaystyle\pi^{(N),c,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right)=\pi^{(N),c,c}_{q^{t},q^{s};u,1}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right).

(224)

Observe that if we send
m\mapsto 4N-r
, then
\theta_{r}\mapsto\pi-\theta_{r}
and thus also
q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}}\mapsto-q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}}
and
q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}}\mapsto q^{{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}}
. These transformations imply that

f_{t;u,\tilde{u}}^{(N),c}(4N-r)=f_{t;\tilde{u},u}^{(N),c}(r),\quad g_{s;u,\tilde{u}}^{(N),c}(4N-m)=g_{s;\tilde{u},u}^{(N),c}(m).

(225)

As in the proof of Lemma
8.3
, by using the transformation (
225
) it will suffice to consider the asymptotics behavior of these
f
and
g
functions just for
m\in[0,2N]
and
r\in[0,2N]
.

Using the notation from
(
172
) and the notation from Section

8.5.2
, we may write

\displaystyle\log\left[\sqrt{1-\frac{m}{4N}}\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{2N}\,\cdot\,\pi^{(N),c,c}_{q^{t},q^{s};u,\tilde{u}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right)\right]=

(226)

\displaystyle-\log\left[4\pi N\sqrt{\frac{r}{N}}\right]+{\mathcal{A}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}(\sqrt{m},\sqrt{r})+{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{{\mathcal{A}}}(m,r)+{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{\mathcal{E}}(m,r).

(227)

The function
{\mathcal{A}}_{N,u,\tilde{u}}(a,b)
is defined for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by

{\mathcal{A}}_{N,u,\tilde{u}}(a,b):={\mathcal{A}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{f}(a,b)+{\mathcal{A}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{g}(a,b)+{\mathcal{A}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{h}(a,b)

(228)

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{f}(a,b):={\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{+}\big[u+\tfrac{t}{2}+{i}\tfrac{b}{2},u+\tfrac{t}{2}-{i}\tfrac{b}{2}\big]+{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{-}\big[\tilde{u}+\tfrac{t}{2}+{i}\tfrac{b}{2},\tilde{u}+\tfrac{t}{2}-{i}\tfrac{b}{2}\big],

(229)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{g}(a,b):={\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{+}\big[{i}a,-{i}a\big]-{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{+}\big[u+\tfrac{s}{2}+{i}\tfrac{b}{2},u+\tfrac{t}{2}-{i}\tfrac{b}{2}\big]-{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{-}\big[\tilde{u}+\tfrac{s}{2}+{i}\tfrac{b}{2},\tilde{u}+\tfrac{s}{2}-{i}\tfrac{b}{2}\big],

(230)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{h}(a,b):={\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{+}\big[1,t-s\big]+{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{-}[u+\tilde{u}+s]-{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{-}[u+\tilde{u}+t]-

(231)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{+}\big[\tfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\tfrac{a+b}{2},\tfrac{t-s}{2}-{i}\tfrac{a+b}{2},\tfrac{t-s}{2}+{i}\tfrac{a-b}{2},\tfrac{t-s}{2}-{i}\tfrac{a-b}{2}\big].

(232)

The error term
{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{\mathcal{A}}(m,r)
is defined as

{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{\mathcal{A}}(m,r):={\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{\mathcal{A},f}(m,r)+{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{\mathcal{A},g}(m,r)+{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{\mathcal{A},h}(m,r),

where, for
\bullet\in\{f,g,h\}
we have

{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}^{\mathcal{A},\bullet}(m,r):=\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}\left(\sqrt{m}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}(m),\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}(r)\right)-\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}(\sqrt{m},\sqrt{r}).

Similarly, we define the error term

{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N,u,\tilde{u}}(m,r):=\mathcal{E}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}\left(\sqrt{m}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(m),\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\right),

where
\mathcal{E}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}(a,b)
is defined exactly as in (
228
), except with the
{\mathcal{A}}
symbol replaced by
{\mathsf{E}}_{N}
, as in (
168
).

Next we use (
225
) and the arguments from Lemma
8.3
to get an
analogue of (
187
), i.e. for all
\eta>1
there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi>0
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
m,r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
, and
\bullet\in\{f,g\}

\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}},\bullet}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}(m,r)\right|,\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}(m,r)\right|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{m})^{\eta}+CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(234)

We can also prove such a bound for
{\mathsf{E}}^{{\mathcal{A}},h}_{N;u,\tilde{u}}(m,r)
directly by using Proposition
2.3
(since
t-s>0
we use the part of the proposition which assumes the condition that
\textup{dist}(\textup{Re}(z),\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0})>r
for some
r>0
).

Using the explicit formulas for
{\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}
from (
37
) and
(
38
), when
\tilde{u}=1
we may simplify the above calculation to see that

\displaystyle-\log\left[4\pi N\sqrt{\frac{r}{N}}\right]+{\mathcal{A}}_{N,u,1}(\sqrt{m},\sqrt{r})

(235)

\displaystyle\qquad=\log\left[\frac{\left|\Gamma\left(u+\frac{s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}}{2},\frac{t-s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{r}}{2},\frac{t-s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}-\sqrt{r}}{2}\right)\right|^{2}}{8\pi\cdot\sqrt{r}\cdot\Gamma(t-s)\left|\Gamma\left(u+\frac{t}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2},{i}\sqrt{m}\right)\right|^{2}}\right].

(236)

There is a
4\pi
factor on the left-hand side above versus
8\pi
on the right-hand side. This extra factor of two comes from an imbalance between the second order expansion in the
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{N}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{N}
.
From Lemma
8.3
, we may write

\log\left[\dfrac{\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)}{\sqrt{1-\frac{m}{4N}}\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)}\right]=\log\left[\dfrac{\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}(r)}{\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{s}(m)}\right]+\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)-\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)

(237)

where the error terms
\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)
and
\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)
are controlled by the bounds in (
126
).
In light of (
216
), we may combine this with (
235
) and (
227
) to conclude that (recall Definition
6.7
)

\displaystyle\log\Big(\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)\Big)=

(238)

\displaystyle\log\left[\frac{\left|\Gamma\left(u+\frac{s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{t-s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{r}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{t-s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}-\sqrt{r}}{2}\right)\right|^{2}}{8\pi\cdot\sqrt{r}\cdot\Gamma(t-s)\left|\Gamma\left(u+\frac{t}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left({i}\sqrt{m}\right)\right|^{2}}\dfrac{\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}(r)}{\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{s}(m)}\right]

(239)

\displaystyle\quad+{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,1}^{{\mathcal{A}}}(m,r)+{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,1}^{\mathcal{E}}(m,r)+\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)-\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)

(240)

\displaystyle=\quad\log\left[\mathfrak{p}^{c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)\right]+\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r),

(241)

where
\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r):={\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,1}^{{\mathcal{A}}}(m,r)+{\mathsf{E}}_{N;u,1}^{\mathcal{E}}(m,r)+\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)-\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)
.
The simplification which produces
\mathfrak{p}^{c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)
above can be verified by appealing to the explicit formula for
\mathfrak{p}^{c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)
from Definition
6.7
.

Combining
237
with (
126
) in Lemma
8.3
(that
|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
and likewise with
r
replaced by
m
) we see that
\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c,c}_{s,t}(m,r)
is likewise bound in absolute value by
CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{m})^{\eta}+CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
which completes the proof of (
139
) and hence part 1 of this lemma.

Part 2.

Since (
143
) follows from (
142
), it remains to prove (
142
).

We may rewrite (
117
) as

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s),r)=\pi^{(N),c,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s))\cdot\dfrac{\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)}{\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{s}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s))},

(242)

where
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
and
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)
, for
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, constitutes all of the atoms in
\mathrm{S}^{(N),d}_{s}
. (Recall that
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)
and
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)
are related by (
132
).)
For
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}
, we may use (
67
) to rewrite, for Borel
V\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\pi^{(N)}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},V\right)=AW\left(V;q^{v+\frac{s}{2}},-q^{1+\frac{s}{2}},q^{\frac{t-s}{2}+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},q^{\frac{t-s}{2}-{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}}\right).

(243)

Based on the discussion about atoms in Section
6.1.1
we observe that as long as
1+\tfrac{s}{2}>0
and
\tfrac{t-s}{2}>0
(both of which necessarily hold since we have assumed
s<t
and
s,t\in(-2,2)
), the only atoms are those coming from the
q^{v+\tfrac{s}{2}}
term. This term has absolute value exceeding
1
and hence we see that the atoms of this measure are precisely
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)
for
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
.

By (
64
), the weight
\pi^{(N),c,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{0}(s)\right)
at
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{0}(s)
is

\dfrac{\left(q^{-2v-s},-q^{1+t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},-q^{1+t/2-{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},q^{t-s}\right)_{\infty}}{\left(-q^{1-v},q^{-v+t/2-s+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},q^{-v+t/2-s-{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},-q^{v+t+1}\right)_{\infty}},

(244)

while, for
j\in\llbracket 1,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
the weights at
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)
are

\pi^{(N),c,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)\right)=\pi^{(N),c,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{0}(s)\right)\times M_{j}

(245)

where the additional multiplicative factor
M_{j}
is defined as

\dfrac{\left(q^{2v+s},-q^{1+v+s},q^{v+t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},q^{v+t/2-{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}}\right)_{j}\left(1-q^{2v+2j+s}\right)}{\left(q,-q^{v},q^{1+v+s-t/2+{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}},q^{1+s+v-t/2-{i}\sqrt{N}\frac{\theta_{r}}{2}}\right)_{j}\left(1-q^{2v+s}\right)}\cdot\left(-q^{-v-t}\right)^{j}.

(246)

In (
244
) and (
246
) there are no instances of division by zero. This would arise in (
244
) if
-v+t/2-s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
and in (
246
) if
1+v+s-t/2+j-1=0
for
j\in\llbracket 1,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, or if
2v+s=0
. However, since
v+s/2<0
and that
s<t
, none of these occur.

Using notation from Section
8.5.2
, we may rewrite (
244
) as

\displaystyle\log\left[\pi^{(N),c,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{0}(s)\right)\right]={\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r})+{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{{\mathcal{A}}}(r)+{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{\mathcal{E}}(r).\quad

(247)

The function
{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(a)
is now (compared to the proof of Lemma
8.3
) defined for
a\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(a):=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{+}\left[-2v-s,t-s\right]+{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{-}\left[1+\dfrac{t}{2}+{i}\dfrac{a}{2},1+\dfrac{t}{2}-{i}\dfrac{a}{2}\right]

(248)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{+}\left[-v+\dfrac{t}{2}-s+{i}\dfrac{a}{2},-v+\dfrac{t}{2}-s-{i}\dfrac{a}{2}\right]-{\mathcal{A}}_{N}^{-}\left[1-v,v+t+1\right].

(249)

The error term

{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{\mathcal{A}}(r):=\mathcal{A}_{N}\left(\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}(r)\right)-\mathcal{A}_{N}(\sqrt{r}).

The error term

{\mathsf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}}_{N}(r):=\mathcal{E}_{N}\left(\sqrt{r}+{\mathsf{E}}^{\theta}_{N}(r)\right),

where
\mathcal{E}_{N}(a)
is defined as in (
249
), except with the
{\mathcal{A}}
replaced by
{\mathsf{E}}_{N}
.

Using the explicit formulas for
{\mathcal{A}}^{\pm}
from (
37
) and (
38
), we may simplify

{\mathcal{A}}_{N}(\sqrt{r})=\log\Bigg[\dfrac{\Big\lvert\Gamma\left(\frac{t}{2}-s-v+\frac{{i}\sqrt{r}}{2}\right)\Big\rvert^{2}}{\Gamma(-2v-s,t-s)}\Bigg].

(250)

From Lemma
8.3
and Lemma
8.4
we may write

\log\left[\dfrac{\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)}{\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{s}\left(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)\right)}\right]=\log\left[\dfrac{\mathfrak{p}^{c}_{t}(r)}{\mathfrak{p}^{d}_{s}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j})}\right]+\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)-\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d}_{s}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(s)),

(251)

where
\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)
and
\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d}_{s}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(s))
are controlled by (
126
) and (
136
).
In light of (
242
), we may combine this with (
250
) and (
247
) to conclude (with Definition
6.7
) that

\log\left[\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{0}(s),r)\right]=\log\left[\mathfrak{p}^{d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0},r)\right]+\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(s),r),

(252)

where
\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(s),r):={\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{{\mathcal{A}}}(r)+{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{\mathcal{E}}(r)+\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),c}_{t}(r)-\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d}_{s}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(s))
.
Just as in the proof of Lemma
8.3
, since Proposition

2.3
can be applied directly we can show that for all
\eta>1
there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi>0
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
,
|\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(s),r)|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
. This completes the proof of (
142
) and hence part 2 of this lemma when
j=0
.

When
j\in\llbracket 1,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
, we must consider
M_{j}
in (
246
). We claim that

M_{j}=\frac{\left[2v+s,v+\frac{t}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2},v+\frac{t}{2}-{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2}\right]_{j}(2v+2j+s)}{\left[1,1+v+s-\tfrac{t}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2},1+v+s-\tfrac{t}{2}-{i}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{2}\right]_{j}(2v+s)}(-1)^{j}\,\cdot\,e^{{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{M_{j}}(r)}

(253)

where
{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{M_{j}}(r)
satisfies the following bound: For all
\eta>1
there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi>0
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
,

\left|{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{M,j}(r)\right|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

(254)

If we combine the above claim with our already established result for
j=0
, we may use Definition
6.7
to match our formula with that of
\mathfrak{p}^{d,c}_{s,t}(x^{u}_{j},r)
so as to conclude that

\log\left[\sqrt{1-\frac{r}{4N}}\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s),r)\right]=\log\left[\mathfrak{p}^{d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j},r)\right]+\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s),r)

(255)

where

\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{j}(s),r):=\widehat{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\mathbf{v}^{v}_{0}(s),r)+{\mathsf{E}}_{N}^{M_{j}}(r)
is bounded in absolute value by
CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}
. This shows (
142
) and hence part 2 of the lemma for
j\in\llbracket 1,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
.

It remains to demonstrate (
253
) with the error bound (
254
).
All of the terms in
M_{j}
which involve
1-q^{a}
,
1+q^{a}
or
q^{a}
for real
a
can be controlled via (
211
) and (
212
), just as in the proof of Lemma
8.4
.
The only terms in
M_{j}
which are not controlled by these bounds are those involving
{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}/2
. To deal with those terms we make use of a more general version of the first growth bound above: there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
z\in\mathbb{C}

\frac{1-q^{z}}{1-q}=z\,\cdot\,e^{{\mathsf{E}}^{4}_{N}(z)}

where the error bound satisfies

\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{4}_{N}(z)\right|\leq CN^{-1/2}(1+|z|).

By combining this bound with the control on
\theta_{r}
demonstrated earlier in Step 2 of the proof of Lemma
8.3
we claim the following bound: for
a\in\mathbb{R}
,

\frac{1-q^{a+{i}\sqrt{N}\theta_{r}/2}}{1-q}=(a+{i}\sqrt{r}/2)\,\cdot\,e^{{\mathsf{E}}^{4}_{N}(a,r)}

(256)

where
{\mathsf{E}}^{4}_{N}(a,r)
satisfies the bound that for any fixed
a
and for all
\eta>1
there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi>0
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and
r\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]
,

\left|{\mathsf{E}}^{4}_{N}(a,r)\right|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{r})^{\eta}.

Combining this bound with (
256
), we can deduce that (
254
) holds and hence complete the proof of this part of the lemma.

Part 3.

We may explicitly write
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s),r)
exactly as in (
242
) except with
u
replacing
v
there. This formula involves the measure
\pi^{(N)}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{r}{2N},V\right)
defined on Borel
V\subset\mathbb{R}
, and (
243
) provides a formula for this measure in terms of the Askey-Wilson measure. We are concerned presently with the atomic part of this measure. However, inspection of the
a,b,c,d
parameters of that measure reveals that as long as
v+\tfrac{s}{2}>0
,
1+\tfrac{s}{2}>0
and
\tfrac{t-s}{2}>0
, this measure has no atomic part. Our assumption in this part is that
u-\tfrac{s}{2}<0
. If
v+\tfrac{s}{2}\leq 0
as well, this would imply that
u+v<0
which violates our assumption that
u+v>0
. Thus, there is no atomic part and so
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s),r)=0
. This completes the proof of this part and hence the entire lemma.

8.5.6
Proof of Lemma
8.6

For parts 1 and 2 of this lemma, let us rewrite (
119
) so that for
x\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
and
y\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d,d}_{s,t}(x,y)=\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(1-\tfrac{y}{2N},1-\tfrac{x}{2N}\right)\cdot\dfrac{\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(y)}{\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{s}\left(x\right)}.

(257)

Part 1.

Since we have assumed that
v+s/2<0
,
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
equals the set of
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)
such that
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
. There are three possibilities for
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
: (1) If
v+t/2<0
then
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
equals the set of
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)
such that
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-t/2\rfloor\rrbracket
; (2) If
v+t/2>0
and
u-t/2>0
then
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
is empty; (3) If
v+t/2>0
and
u-t/2<0
then
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
contains
u
-atoms
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)
. However, this third possibility is excluded since the condition
v+s/2
implies that
u>0
and by our assumption that
t<\mathsf{C}_{u,v}
it follows that
u-t/2>0
(recall when
u>0
,
\mathsf{C}_{u,v}=\min(2u,2)
so
t<\mathsf{C}_{u,v}
means
t<2u
). The case of empty support
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
requires no further argument, so from here on out we assume that we are in the first case where
v+t/2<0
.

In Lemma
8.4
we have already controlled the convergence of the discrete marginal distribution masses. So, recalling that
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)
and
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)
are related by (
132
), our problem now reduces to studying the behavior of
\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}
. We may use (
67
) to rewrite, for any Borel subset
V\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\pi^{(N)}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t),V\right)=AW\left(V;q^{v+\frac{s}{2}},-q^{1+\frac{s}{2}},q^{\frac{t-s}{2}+v+k+\frac{t}{2}},q^{\frac{t-s}{2}-(v+k+\frac{t}{2})}\right).

(258)

We will analyze the probability masses in (
258
) when
V=\{\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)\}
for
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
. The support of the measure
\pi^{(N)}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t),V\right)
may actually include more atoms that just this set, namely coming from the fact that
q^{\frac{t-s}{2}+v+k+\frac{t}{2}}
may have absolute value exceeding
1
. We do not, however, need to consider these atoms since in (
257
) we are restricting ourselves to having the first variable
x\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
, which does not include these additional atoms.

We use (
64
) to write out these masses. The weight at
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{0}(s)
is

\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t),\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{0}(s)\right)=\frac{\left(q^{-2v-s},-q^{v+k+1+t},q^{t-s},-q^{1-v-k}\right)_{\infty}}{\left(-q^{1-v},q^{k+t-s},q^{-2v-k-s},-q^{v+t+1}\right)_{\infty}}

(259)

while, for
j\in\llbracket 1,\lfloor-v-s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
the weights at
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)
are

\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t),\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{j}(s)\right)=\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t),\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{0}(s)\right)\times M_{j}

(260)

where the additional multiplicative factor
M_{j}
is defined as

M_{j}:=\dfrac{\left(q^{2v+s},-q^{1+v+s},q^{2v+k+t},q^{-k}\right)_{j}\left(1-q^{2v+2j+s}\right)}{\left(q,-q^{v},q^{1-k-t+s},q^{1+2v+k+s}\right)_{j}\left(1-q^{2v+s}\right)}\cdot\left(-q^{-v-t}\right)^{j}.

(261)

The analysis of these formulas follows the same approach as the earlier lemmas, for example Part 2 of Lemma
8.5
. In fact, since all exponents are real, the analysis and control of error terms is even simpler. As such, we just record the limiting expressions which arise from applying Proposition
2.3
. Observe that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t),\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{0}(s)\right)=\frac{\Gamma\left(k+t-s,-2v-k-s\right)}{\Gamma\left(-2v-s,t-s\right)}

(262)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}M_{j}=\frac{\left[2v+s,2v+k+t,-k\right]_{j}(2v+2j+s)}{\left[1,1-k-t+s,1+2v+k+s\right]_{j}(2v+s)}(-1)^{j}.

(263)

Putting these expressions together with our knowledge of the asymptotic behavior of
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{k}(t))
and
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{s}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{j}(s))
, we readily confirm the expansion (
145
) and error bound (
146
).

Part 2.

Since we have assumed that
u-s/2<0
,
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
equals the set of
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s)
such that
j\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+s/2\rfloor\rrbracket
. Since
s<t
it follows that
u-t/2<0
as well thus
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
equals the set of
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)
such that
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+t/2\rfloor\rrbracket
. As we will see, the transition mass from
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{j}(s)
to
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)
is zero when
j<k
.

In Lemma
8.4
we have already controlled the convergence of the discrete marginal distribution masses. So, recalling that
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)
and
\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)
are related by (
132
), our problem now reduces to studying the behavior of
\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}
. We may use (
67
) to rewrite, for any Borel subset
V\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\pi^{(N)}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(t),V\right)=AW\left(V;q^{v+\frac{s}{2}},-q^{1+\frac{s}{2}},q^{\frac{t-s}{2}+u+k-\frac{t}{2}},q^{\frac{t-s}{2}-(u+k-\frac{t}{2})}\right).

(264)

Atoms in the Askey-Wilson measure on the right-hand side of (
264
) arise when arguments exceed
1
in absolute value. Since
u-s/2<0
, it follows (since
u+v>0
) that
v+s/2>0
so
|q^{v+\frac{s}{2}}|<1
. Likewise, since we have assumed that
s>-2
,
|-q^{1+\frac{s}{2}}|<1
. The fourth argument necessarily satisfies
|q^{\frac{t-s}{2}-(u+k-\frac{t}{2})}|
because for
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+\frac{t}{2}\rfloor\rrbracket
, we have that
t-s-u+\frac{s}{2}-k\geq\frac{t-s}{2}>0
. The absolute value of the third argument
|q^{\frac{t-s}{2}+u+k-\frac{t}{2}}|
can exceed
1
if
u+k-\frac{s}{2}<0
. In that case, this term will contribute atoms at
\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k+i}(s)
for
i\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+\frac{s}{2}-k\rfloor\rrbracket
, which is a subset of
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
. For all other elements in
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{s}
, there will be no atom in this transition probability.

It remains to compute the masses of the atoms in the Askey-Wilson process (
258
) at
\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k+i}(s)
for
i\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+\frac{s}{2}-k\rfloor\rrbracket
. For this we use (
64
), noting that in this case we have to switch the
a
and
c
arguments in the formula for the masses. This yields

\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(t),\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(s)\right)=\frac{\left(q^{-2u-2k+s},-q^{v+1+s},q^{v-u-k+t},-q^{1-u-k+t}\right)_{\infty}}{\left(q^{v-u-k+s},-q^{1-u-k+s},q^{-2u-2k+t},-q^{v+t+1}\right)_{\infty}},

(265)

while for
i\in\llbracket 1,\lfloor-u+\frac{s}{2}-k\rfloor\rrbracket

\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(t),\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k+i}(s)\right)=\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(t),\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(s)\right)\times M_{i}

(266)

where the additional multiplicative factor
M_{i}
is defined as

M_{i}=\frac{\left(q^{2u+2k-s},q^{v+u+k},-q^{u+1+k},q^{t-s}\right)_{i}\left(1-q^{2u+2k+2i-s}\right)}{\left(q,q^{u-v+1+k-s},-q^{u+k-s},q^{2u+2k-t+1}\right)_{i}\left(1-q^{2u+2k-s}\right)}\cdot\left(-q^{-v-2t+s}\right)^{i}.

(267)

The analysis of these formulas follows the same approach as the earlier lemmas, for example Part 2 of Lemma
8.5
. In fact, since all exponents are real, the analysis and control of error terms is even simpler. As such, we just record the limiting expressions which arise from applying Proposition
2.3
.
Observe that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\pi^{(N),d,d}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(t),\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(s)\right)=\frac{\Gamma\left(v-u-k+s\right)\Gamma\left(-2u-2k+t\right)}{\Gamma\left(-2u-2k+s\right)\Gamma\left(v-u-k+t\right)}

(268)

and that

\lim_{N\to\infty}M_{i}=\frac{\left[2u+2k-s,v+u+k,t-s\right]_{i}(2u+2k+2i-s)}{\left[1,u-v+1+k-s,2u+2k-t+1\right]_{j}(2u+2k-s)}(-1)^{i}.

(269)

Putting these expressions together with our knowledge of the asymptotic behavior of
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t))
and
\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{s}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k+i}(s))
, we readily confirm the expansion (
148
)
and error bound (
149
).

Part 3.

First consider the case when
u-t/2<0
. In this case, the discrete support of the measure
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}(m,\cdot)
is given by
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
which equals the set of
\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)
such that
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-u+t/2\rfloor\rrbracket
. We may rewrite (
118
) as

\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)\right)=\frac{1}{2N}\,\cdot\,\pi^{(N),d,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(t),1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right)\cdot\dfrac{\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)\right)}{\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)}.

(270)

We have already studied the behavior of the marginal distribution terms on the right-hand side above, thus we focus now on
\pi^{(N),d,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(t),1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right)
.
In (
264
) we rewrote this in terms of the Askey-Wilson process. The density of the absolutely continuous part of that measure is given in (
61
) and using that expression and mimicking the asymptotic analysis in the proof of Lemma
8.5
, we show that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{1-\frac{m}{4N}}}{2N}\,\cdot\,\pi^{(N),d,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{u}^{(N),u}_{k}(t),1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right)=

(271)

\displaystyle\dfrac{\Big\lvert\Gamma\left(u+\frac{s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}}{2},u+j-\frac{s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}}{2},-u-j+t-\frac{s}{2}+{i}\frac{\sqrt{m}}{2}\right)\Big\rvert^{2}}{8\pi\cdot\sqrt{m}\cdot\Gamma(v+u+j,v-u-j+t,t-s)\cdot\bigg\rvert\Gamma\left({i}\sqrt{m}\right)\bigg\rvert^{2}}\cdot e^{\widetilde{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(m,\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t))}

(272)

and that for all

\eta>1
, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
C,\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for all
N>N_{0}
and all
m\in\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),c}=[0,4N]

\Big|\widetilde{\mathsf{Err}}^{(N),d,c}_{s,t}(m,\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t))\Big|\leq CN^{-\chi}(1+\sqrt{m})^{\eta}.

(273)

In light of (
270
), combining the above error bound with our bounds on

\hat{\pi}^{(N),d}_{t}\left(\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)\right)
and
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c}_{s}(m)
from Lemma
8.4
and
8.3
, we arrive at the claimed result from the lemma. Note that the factor of
\sqrt{1-\frac{m}{4N}}
cancels with a corresponding factor coming from our application of Lemma
8.3
and that the matching to
\mathfrak{p}^{c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\mathbf{u}^{u}_{k}(t)\right)
can be seen from Definition
6.7
.

Now let us turn to the case when
v+t/2<0
. In this case, the discrete support of the measure
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}(m,\cdot)
is given by
\mathrm{\hat{S}}^{(N),d}_{t}
which equals the set of
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{(N),v}_{k}(t)
such that
k\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor-v-t/2\rfloor\rrbracket
. We may rewrite (
118
) as in (
270
) with
u
replaced by
v
. Focusing on
\pi^{(N),d,c}_{q^{t},q^{s}}\left(\mathbf{v}^{(N),v}_{k}(t),1-\tfrac{m}{2N}\right)
we see that the explicit expression for this include
q^{-k}
inside the
q
-Pochhammer symbols in the numerator. This implies that the numerator is zero. By inspect, the denominator is non zero. This implies that
\hat{\pi}^{(N),c,d}_{s,t}\left(m,\hat{\mathbf{u}}^{(N),u}_{k}(t)\right)
is identically zero, completing the proof of part 3 and hence the lemma.

9
Asymptotics of
(\pm q^{z};q)_{\infty}
: Proof of Proposition
2.3

Throughout we will use the notation
s=\sigma+{i}t
and let
\textup{Arg(s)}\in[-\pi,\pi]
denote the argument of the complex number
s
.

9.1
Preliminaries from analytic number theory

9.1.1
Gamma function

For
s\in\mathbb{C}
with
\textup{Re}(s)>1
define the gamma function as

\Gamma(s):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x}x^{s-1}dx.

This can be meromorphically continuated with

\textup{simple poles at }\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\textup{ and }\textup{Res}_{s=-k}\left[\Gamma(s)\right]=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\textup{ for }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}.

(274)

The Euler reflection formula shows that

\Gamma(1-s)\Gamma(s)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi s)}.

For
s=\sigma+it
,
[
47
, (21.51)]
shows that:

Lemma 9.1

For any compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}
and
{\varepsilon}>0
there exists
t_{0}>0
such that for all
\sigma\in K
and
t
with
|t|>t_{0}
,

\left|\frac{\big|\Gamma(s)\big|}{\sqrt{2\pi}|t|^{\sigma-1/2}e^{-\pi|t|/2}}-1\right|<{\varepsilon}.

(275)

9.1.2
Zeta and eta functions

(See
[
40
, Sections 1.3–1.4]
.) The Riemann zeta function
\zeta(s)
is defined for
\textup{Re}(s)>1
as

\zeta(s):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{s}}

and is the
z=1
specialization of the Hurwitz zeta function
\zeta(s,z)
, which is defined for
\textup{Re}(s)>1
and
\textup{Re}(z)>0
as

\zeta(s,z):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n+z)^{s}}.

(276)

Still assuming
\textup{Re}(s)>1
and
\textup{Re}(z)>0
, the Hurwitz zeta function admits an integral representation as

\zeta(s,z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\rho z}\rho^{s-1}}{1-e^{-\rho}}d\rho.

(277)

As functions of
s
, both
\zeta(s)
and
\zeta(s,z)
can be meromorphically extended to the complex plane and yield meromorphic functions having simple poles only at
s=1
with residues

\textup{Res}_{s=1}\left[\zeta(s)\right]=\textup{Res}_{s=1}\left[\zeta(s,z)\right]=1.

(278)

We will use of the following evaluation formulas for the zeta function

\zeta(2)=\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{6},\qquad\zeta(0,z)=\dfrac{1}{2}-z,

(279)

as well various derivatives and limits

\displaystyle\dfrac{d\zeta(s,z)}{ds}\bigg\lvert_{s=0}\!\!=\log\left[\dfrac{\Gamma(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right],

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}(0)=-\dfrac{1}{2}\log(2\pi),

(280)

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow 1}{\zeta(s,z)-\dfrac{1}{s-1}}=-\psi(z),

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow 1}{\zeta(s)-\dfrac{1}{s-1}}=-\psi(1).

(281)

Above we have used the digamma function
\psi(z)=\dfrac{\Gamma^{\prime}(z)}{\Gamma(z)}
whose evaluation
\psi(1)=-\gamma
is given by the Euler-Mascheroni constant
\gamma
.

Finally, the Dirichlet eta function
\eta(s)
is defined for
\textup{Re}(s)>0
as

\eta(s):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n^{s}},

and is related to the zeta function via

\eta(s)=(1-2^{1-s})\zeta(s).

(282)

From this one sees that the eta function is an entire function (the
1-2^{1-s}
factor cancels the first order pole of the zeta function at
s=1
).

9.1.3
Bernoulli polynomials

The Bernoulli polynomials
\big\{B_{n}(x)\big\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
are be defined via the generating function expression

\frac{te^{tx}}{e^{t}-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}B_{n}(x)\frac{t^{n}}{n!}.

In particular,
B_{n}(x)
is a degree
n
polynomial in
x
with the first few polynomials given by
B_{0}(x)=1
,
B_{1}(x)=x-\tfrac{1}{2}
,
B_{2}(x)=x^{2}-x+\tfrac{1}{6}
, and so on. We recall some results we will need from
[
2
, Sections 12.11-12.12]
.
The Bernoulli polynomials satisfy

B_{n}(1-x)=(-1)^{n}B_{n}(x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{n}(x+1)-B_{n}(x)=nx^{n-1},

(283)

which implies that, through taking
x=0
,
B_{n}(1)=B_{n}(0)
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
and
B_{1}(1)=\tfrac{1}{2}=-B_{1}(0)
.
The Bernoulli numbers are defined as

B_{n}:=B_{n}(0).

Besides
B_{1}=-\tfrac{1}{2}
, all other odd indexed Bernoulli numbers are
0
.

In terms of the Bernoulli polynomials, we have that for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

\zeta(-n,z)=-\frac{B_{n+1}(z)}{n+1},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\zeta(-n)=(-1)^{n}\frac{B_{n+1}}{n+1}.

(284)

9.1.4
Asymptotics of
\zeta(s)
and
\zeta(s,z)

We will need the following asymptotic result for the Riemann zeta function which can be found in
[
47
, Section 43]
. For
\sigma\in[0,1]
, the bound proven below is suboptimal, though sufficient for our purposes. The Lindelöf function
\mu(\sigma)
determines the optimal growth exponent. The Lindelöf hypothesis posits that
\mu(1/2)=0
, though this is far from proved. We use
\tilde{\mu}(\sigma)
below to represent an upper bound on this exponent as
\sigma
varies.

Lemma 9.2

For any compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}
, there exists a
C,t_{0}>0
such that

\lvert\zeta(s)\rvert\leq C|t|^{\tilde{\mu}(\sigma)}

for all
s=\sigma+{i}t
with
\sigma\in K
and
|t|>t_{0}
. Here
\tilde{\mu}(\sigma)
is defined by

\tilde{\mu}(\sigma)=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{for }\sigma\geq 1,\\
\frac{1-\sigma}{2}&\textrm{for }\sigma\in[0,1],\\
\frac{1}{2}-\sigma&\textrm{for }\sigma\leq 0.\end{cases}

Next, we prove a simple bound on the Hurwitz zeta function.

Lemma 9.3

For all
\sigma_{0}>1
there exists a
C>0
such that for all
s
with
\sigma>\sigma_{0}
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\textup{Re}(z)>0
, we have

|\zeta(s,z)|\leq Ce^{|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}.

(285)

Proof.

From (
276
) and the triangle inequality,
|\zeta(s,z)|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|(n+z)^{-s}|
. Note that

|(n+z)^{-s}|=|n+z|^{-\sigma}e^{\textup{Arg}(n+z)\cdot t}
. Since
\textup{Re}(z)>0
,
|n+z|>|n|
and
|\textup{Arg}(n+z)|<|\textup{Arg}(z)|
. Thus we can further bound
|\zeta(s,z)|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|n|^{-\sigma}e^{|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}\leq Ce^{|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}

where the constant
C
can be taken as
1+\zeta(\sigma_{0})
.
∎

Controlling
|\zeta(s,z)|
when
\textup{Re}(s)\leq 1
is considerably hard. It will be important to demonstrate bounds in that case which contain the
z
dependence on the sub-leading polynomial terms. Such bounds are provided below as Proposition
9.5
. In the proof of the proposition, we will make use of an integral formulas for
\zeta(s,z)
. There are many related formulas available in the literature (cf.
[
40
, Section 1.4]
). We could note find a precise statement of the formula in Lemma
9.4
, thus we prove it here.

Lemma 9.4

For
s=\sigma+{i}t\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
,
|\textup{Arg}(z)|<\pi
and
d\in(0,1)
with
\sigma+d\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
,

\displaystyle\zeta(s,z)=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2z^{s}}+\dfrac{z^{1-s}}{s-1}+\dfrac{z^{-s}}{2\pi{i}\Gamma(s)}\int\limits^{d+{i}\infty}_{d-{i}\infty}\Gamma(-u,u+s)z^{-u}\zeta(-u)du

(286)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{z^{-s}}{\Gamma(s)}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\lfloor-(\sigma+d)\rfloor}\Gamma(k+s)\zeta(k+s)\frac{(-1)^{k}z^{k+s}}{k!},

(287)

(Recall
\Gamma(a,b)=\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)
) where the summation in
k
is dropped if
\sigma+d>0
.

Proof.

We will make use of the following formula
[
46
, (3.3.9)]

\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{c-{i}\infty}^{c+{i}\infty}\Gamma(s,a-s)x^{-s}ds=\frac{\Gamma(a)}{(1+x)^{a}},

(288)

which is valid so long as
\textup{Re}(a)>c>0
and
|\textup{Arg}(x)|<\pi
.

Start by assuming
\sigma>1
,
|\textup{Arg}(z)|<\pi
and
c\in(1,\sigma)
. Then from the definition of
\zeta(s,z)
(this formula
appears as in
[
45
, (2.1)]
)

\displaystyle z^{s}\zeta(s,z)=1+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1+\frac{n}{z}\right)^{-s}=1+\dfrac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{c-{i}\infty}^{c+{i}\infty}\Gamma(y,s-y)\left(\frac{n}{z}\right)^{-y}dy

(289)

\displaystyle=1+\int\limits_{c-{i}\infty}^{c+{i}\infty}\frac{\Gamma(y,s-y)z^{y}\zeta(y)dy}{2\pi{i}\Gamma(s)}=1+\int\limits_{-c-{i}\infty}^{-c+{i}\infty}\frac{\Gamma(-u,s+u)z^{-u}\zeta(-u)du}{2\pi{i}\Gamma(s)}

(290)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}+\dfrac{z}{s-1}+\int\limits_{d-{i}\infty}^{d+{i}\infty}\frac{\Gamma(-u,s+u)z^{u}\zeta(-u)du}{2\pi{i}\Gamma(s)},

(291)

for
d\in(0,1)
.
The first equality is by the definition of the Hurwitz zeta function; the second is by (
288
); the third is from interchanging the summation and integration (justified by Fubini) and appealing to the definition of the zeta function (we assume
\sigma>c>1
here); the fourth equality is the simple change of variables
u=-y
; and the final equality follows from shifting the contour of integration to the right from
-c+{i}\mathbb{R}
to
d+{i}\mathbb{R}
for
d\in(0,1)
. In this shifting, we encounter two poles, one at
u=-1
(from
\zeta(-u)
) and one at
u=0
(from
\Gamma(-u)
). The first two terms in the final line come from evaluating these residues, see (
274
) and (
278
). To justify shifting the contours we must show that the integrand decays sufficiently fast for
|\textup{Im}(u)|
large. Using the bounds from Lemmas
9.1
and
9.2
we can prove uniformly in the strip between
-c+{i}\mathbb{R}
and
d+{i}\mathbb{R}
, exponential decay like
e^{(|\textup{Arg}(z)|-\pi)|\textup{Im}(u)|}
(recall that
|\textup{Arg}(z)|<\pi
) as
|\textup{Im}(u)|\to\infty
.

We have shown that for
|\textup{Arg}(z)|<\pi
,
\sigma>1
and any
d\in(0,1)
:

\zeta(s,z)=\dfrac{1}{2z^{s}}+\dfrac{z^{1-s}}{s-1}+\dfrac{z^{-s}}{2\pi{i}\Gamma(s)}\int\limits^{d+{i}\infty}_{d-{i}\infty}\Gamma(-u,u+s)z^{-u}\zeta(-u)du.

(292)

By (
280
) it follows that
\zeta(s,z)-\dfrac{z^{1-s}}{s-1}
is an entire function. For a fixed value of
d\in(0,1)
, the integral in (
292
) is analytic in
s
provided
\sigma+d>0
. By analytic continuation, the formula (
292
) actually holds for all
\sigma>-d
.

In order to extend to a formula for
\sigma\leq-d
we will need to make some contour deformations and account for some residues.

Our aim is now to establish a formula for
s=\sigma+{i}t
when
\sigma<-d
. For the moment, let us assume that
t\neq 0
and let us fix some
d\in(0,1)
and assume that
\sigma<-d
and that
\sigma+d\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. Fix some
\tilde{\sigma}<-d
and let
\varepsilon_{0}=|t|/2
(which is non-zero by our temporary assumption) and
\varepsilon_{1}=(\lceil-\tilde{\sigma}\rceil+\tilde{\sigma})/2
. By Cauchy’s theorem without changing the value of the integral we can deform to the contour
C
:

\begin{split}C=&[d-{i}\infty,d-{i}(t+\varepsilon_{0})]\cup[d-{i}(t+\varepsilon_{0}),\varepsilon_{1}-\tilde{\sigma}-{i}(t+\varepsilon_{0})]\cup\\
&[\varepsilon_{1}-\tilde{\sigma}-{i}(t+\varepsilon_{0}),\varepsilon_{1}-\tilde{\sigma}-{i}(t-\varepsilon_{0})]\cup\\
&[\varepsilon_{1}-\tilde{\sigma}-{i}(t-\varepsilon_{0}),d-{i}(t-\varepsilon_{0})]\cup[d-{i}(t-\varepsilon_{0}),d+{i}\infty].\end{split}

(293)

The purpose of this deformation is that the integral is now analytic in
s
provided that
\sigma\geq\tilde{\sigma}
. Let us now assume that
\sigma=\tilde{\sigma}
. To reach a final formula we will deform
C
back to the original contour
d+{i}\mathbb{R}
. In doing so, we cross poles from the
\Gamma(u+s)
term. These occur when
u+s=-k
for
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor-(\sigma+d)\rfloor\}
. Taking into account the residues and the direction of the contours yields (
287
)
when
t\neq 0
. Provided that
s\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, we can use continuity of both side of (
287
) in
t
to extend to
t=0
.
∎

We come to our main bound on the Hurwitz zeta function.

Proposition 9.5

For any non-integer
\sigma<0
and
d\in(0,1/2)
chosen such that

d+\sigma\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, there exists
C>0
such that for all

z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\textup{Arg}(z)|<\pi
and all
s\in\mathbb{C}
with
s=\sigma+{i}t
,

\displaystyle\qquad|\zeta(s,z)|\leq C\Big(e^{\lvert\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t\rvert}\left(|z|^{-\sigma}+(1+|t|)^{-1}|z|^{1-\sigma}+|t|^{1/2-\sigma}|z|^{-d-\sigma}\right)\qquad

(294)

\displaystyle+\max(1,|t|^{1/2-\sigma})\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\lfloor-(\sigma+d)\rfloor}|z|^{k}\Big),

(295)

where the summation in
k
is dropped if
\sigma+d>0
.

For any
t_{0}>0
,
a<b
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\textup{Arg}(z)|<\pi
, there exists a constant
C>0
and
c<\pi/2
such that for all
s\in\mathbb{C}
with
\sigma\in(a,b)
and
|t|\geq t_{0}
,

|\zeta(s,z)|\leq Ce^{c|t|}.

(296)

Proof.

We focus on proving (
294
). The proof of the bound (
296
) is simpler and proceeds in much the same manner (and thus is not provided here).

In this proof when we write
x\lesssim y
we mean that
x\leq Cy
for
some constant which may depend on
d
and
\sigma
, but nothing else. In turn, when we say that “
x
is bounded by
y
”, we mean that
x\lesssim y
and when we say that “
x
is bounded”, we mean that
x
is bounded by a constant. We also will make use of Lemmas
9.1
and
9.2
to deduce bounds when the imaginary part of the argument of the gamma or zeta function is small or large. For the rest of this proof, let
t_{0}
be such that the bound in Lemma
9.1
holds for
{\varepsilon}=1/2
, and such that the bound in Lemma
9.2
holds for some constant
C
as specified in the lemma. We will use
t_{0}
as the cutoff between small and large.

This proof relies on the integral representation for
\zeta(s,z)
given in Lemma
9.4
. The hardest term to bound in that representation is the contour integral. Let us address the other terms first.
For the first two terms in the
\zeta(s,z)
representation in Lemma
9.4
, we find that

\left|\frac{1}{2z^{s}}\right|\lesssim e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}|z|^{-\sigma}\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\frac{z^{1-s}}{s-1}\right|\lesssim e^{-\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}(1+|t|)^{-1}|z|^{1-\sigma},

where, in both terms we have used the fact that

|z^{s}|=|z|^{\sigma}e^{-\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}
,
and in the second inequality we use that for
s
with negative real part,
|1/(1-s)|<C(1+|t|)^{-1}
for some constant
C
. Since
e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}
and
e^{-\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}
are both bounded by
e^{|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}
, we find that the contribution of these two terms is upper bounded by the first two terms in the right-hand side of (
294
).

The
\zeta(s,z)
representation in Lemma
9.4
also involves terms indexed by
k\in\{0,\ldots,\lfloor-(\sigma+d)\rfloor\}
. Taking absolute values these terms contribute a constant times

|\Gamma(s)|^{-1}\,\cdot\,|\Gamma(k+s)|\,\cdot\,|\zeta(k+s)||z|^{k}.

For large
t
, appealing to the asymptotics of Lemmas

9.1
and
9.2
, we can show that the
expression above is bounded by
|t|^{1/2-\sigma}|z|^{k}
where as for

t
small, since we have assumed that
s
(and hence also
s+k
) is
not in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the expression is bounded by
|z|^{k}
. These bounds produce the final terms in (
294
).

All that remains is to control the contour integral term in (
287
).
Taking the absolute value inside of the integral, we are left to control

\left|\dfrac{z^{-s}}{2\pi{i}\Gamma(s)}\right|\int\limits^{\infty}_{-\infty}|\Gamma(-d-{i}r,d+\sigma+{i}(r+t))|\cdot|z^{-d-{i}r}|\cdot|\zeta(-d-{i}r)dr|.

(297)

The rest of this proof is devoted to showing that (
297
) is bounded by the right-hand side of (
294
). This is elementary, though requires the analysis of a number of cases and the use of the bounds from Lemmas
9.1
and
9.2
for large imaginary parts of the gamma and zeta functions, as well as constant bounds on the gamma and zeta functions for small imaginary parts (for the gamma function, this is where
d+\sigma\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
is important).

We split our analysis of (
297
) into two cases —
|t|\leq t_{0}
and
|t|>t_{0}
.

Case 1:
|t|\leq t_{0}
.
Using Lemma
9.1
and the analyticity of
1/\Gamma(s)
we bound the pre-factor

\left|\dfrac{z^{-s}}{2\pi{i}\Gamma(s)}\right|\lesssim|z|^{-\sigma}e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}\lesssim|z|^{-\sigma}e^{|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}

(298)

Using this and
|z^{-d-{i}r}|=|z|^{-d}e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
inside the integrand of (
297
) yields

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:toboundd2}\lesssim(I)\times(II),\quad\textrm{where}\quad(I):=|z|^{-\sigma-d}\,\cdot\,e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}\quad\textrm{and}

(299)

\displaystyle(II):=\int\limits^{\infty}_{-\infty}|\Gamma(-d-{i}r,d+\sigma+{i}(r+t))|\,\cdot\,e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}\,\cdot\,|\zeta(-d-{i}r)\,dr|.

(300)

We claim that
(II)\lesssim 1
. Assume this claim for the moment. Since
d\in(0,1/2)
,
|z|^{-\sigma-d}\leq(|z|^{-\sigma}+|z|^{1-\sigma})
. Thus
(I)\times(II)\lesssim e^{|\textup{Arg}(z)|}(|z|^{-\sigma}+|z|^{1-\sigma})
which is, itself, bounded by the right-hand side of (
294
) as desired.

To bound
(II)\lesssim 1
, we split the integral into
|r|\leq t_{0}

and
|r|>t_{0}
. In the first case, since the integrands can be bounded
by constants, the total contribution is likewise bounded by a
constant. In the second case, to estimate the integral over
|r|>t_{0}
,
we may use of Lemmas
9.1
and
9.2
for
the gamma and zeta functions. The integrand in
(II)
is thus bounded up to a constant factor
by

|r|^{\sigma+d-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
. Since
\sigma<0
and
d\in(0,1/2)
,
\sigma+d-\frac{1}{2}<0

and hence
|r|^{\sigma+d-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim 1
for
|r|>t_{0}
. Since

|\textup{Arg}(z)|<\pi
and
|t|\leq t_{0}
, for
r
large enough,
-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r<-\delta r
for some
\delta>0
. Thus the integral in
(II)
can be bounded by a constant as claimed.

Case 2:
|t|>t_{0}
.
We proceed in a similar, albeit more involved, manner as in Case 1. In place of the bound (
298
) we get (using Lemma
9.1
to control the behavior of
|1/\Gamma(s)|
) that

\left|\dfrac{z^{-s}}{2\pi{i}\Gamma(s)}\right|\lesssim|z|^{-\sigma}e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}|t|^{-\sigma+1/2}e^{\pi|t|/2}.

(301)

As opposed to in Case 1, we do not want to throw away the possible decay that
e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}
can provide. Instead, we write this as
e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}=e^{|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}\,\cdot\,e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t-|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}
. The first term goes with
(I)
below, while the second term goes with
(II)
.
Using
|z^{-d-{i}r}|=|z|^{-d}e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
we see that

\eqref{eq:toboundd2}\lesssim(I)\times(II)\quad\textrm{where}\quad(I):=|z|^{-\sigma-d}\,\cdot\,e^{|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}\,\cdot\,|t|^{-\sigma+\frac{1}{2}}\quad\textrm{and}

(II):=e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t-|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|+\frac{\pi}{2}|t|}\int\limits^{\infty}_{-\infty}F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)dr.

with

F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)=|\Gamma(-d-{i}r,d+\sigma+{i}(r+t))|e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}|\zeta(-d-{i}r)|

We claim that
(II)\lesssim 1
. Assuming this we see that
(I)\cdot(II)
is bounded by the right hand side in (
294
). Thus, it remains to show that
(II)\lesssim 1
.

In order to bound
(II)
, we split the integral depending on the size of
r
and
r+t
. Assume that
t>t_{0}
(the case
t<-t_{0}
is completely analogous and involve the primed regions in the figure; we will not repeat the argument in that case though). We define six regions in the
(t,r)
plane:

A=\{(t,r):t>t_{0},r>t_{0}\}
,

B=\{(t,r):t>t_{0},|r|\leq t_{0},r+t>t_{0}\}
,

C=\{(t,r):t>t_{0},|r|\leq t_{0},|r+t|\leq t_{0}\}
,

D=\{(t,r):t>t_{0},r<-t_{0},r+t>t_{0}\}
,

E=\{(t,r):t>t_{0},r<-t_{0},|r+t|\leq t_{0}\}
,

F=\{(t,r):t>t_{0},r<-t_{0},t+r<-t_{0}\}
.
For
t
given, we write
(II)_{A}
to denote the expression given above for
(II)
subject to the additional restriction that
(r,r+t)\in A
(and likewise for
B,C,D,E,F
).

For a fixed
t
in each region
A,B,C,D,E,F
we may upper bound the integrand
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)
defining
(II)
either by constants if the imaginary part of the argument of
the gamma or zeta function is small, or by the asymptotics given in
Lemmas
9.1
and
9.2
if the imaginary
part of the argument of the gamma or zeta function are large. We can
then estimate the contribution of each region to the
integral. Depending on whether
t\in(t_{0},2t_{0}]
or
t>2t_{0}
, the integral in
r
will encounter a different set of regions. We consider these two cases.

Case 2.a:
t\in(t_{0},2t_{0}]
.

Here
(II)=(II)_{A}+(II)_{B}+(II)_{C}+(II)_{E}+(II)_{F}
. On
regions
B,C
and
E
the integrand
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim 1
and
since the domain of integration for
r
in these regions is bounded, it follows that
(II)_{B},(II)_{C},(II)_{E}\lesssim 1
. Regions
A
and
F
involve unbounded integrals and require
close inspection. On region
A
,
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
. We already
encountered such an integral in Case 1 when bounding
(II)
there.
Since
\sigma<0
and
d\in(0,1/2)
,
\sigma+d-\frac{1}{2}<0

and hence
|r+t|^{\sigma+d-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim 1
. Since

|\textup{Arg}(z)|<\pi
and
t\in(t_{0},2t_{0}]
, for
r
large enough,

-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r<-\delta r
for some

\delta>0
. Since
t\in(t_{0},2t_{0}]
the prefactor
e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t-|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|+\frac{\pi}{2}|t|}
in front of the
integral in
(II)
is also bounded by a constant. Thus,
(II)_{A}\lesssim 1
as desired. The
F
region follows similarly, as the integrand
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
. In both of these cases of region
A
and
F
we have used the fact that
t\in(t_{0},2t_{0}]
to bound the term
e^{\mp\frac{\pi}{2}t}
in the prefactor by a constant. In the next case, this will not be true.

Case 2.b:
t>2t_{0}
.

Now
(II)=(II)_{A}+(II)_{B}+(II)_{D}+(II)_{E}+(II)_{F}
. As in Case 2.a, for
(r,r+t)\in A
,
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
. The term
|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim 1
, and the
e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}\lesssim e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}t-\delta\cdot r}
for some
\delta>0
. This implies that the integral of
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)
over
r
such that
(r,r+t)\in A
is bounded by a constant times
e^{-\pi t/2}
. This cancels the
e^{\pi|t|/2}
pre-factor outside the integral in
(II)
. What is left is bounded by a constant times
e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t-|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}
and since
\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t-|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|\leq 0
, we conclude that
(II)_{A}\lesssim 1
.

In region
B
, the integrand
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|r+t|+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
. Since
r+t>t_{0}
in this region, and since the
r
-variable is integrated from
-t_{0}
to
t_{0}
, the contribution of the integral of
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)
for
r
in this region is bounded by a constant time
e^{-\pi t/2}
. Again, this cancels the
e^{\pi|t|/2}
pre-factor outside the integral in
(II)
and thus
(II)_{B}\lesssim 1
.
Bounding the integral in regions
D,E
and
F
is more subtle.

Let us start by addressing
(II)_{D}
. Here,

F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}.

Since
r\in(-t+t_{0},-t_{0})
,
|r|=-r
and
|r+t|=r+t
. Also, since
d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}<0
,
|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}
is bounded by a constant. Thus, the upper bound on the integrand
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)
reduces to
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}t}e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
. The magnitude of the integral
\int_{-t+1}^{-1}e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}dr
depends on the sign of
\textup{Arg}(z)
.
If
\textup{Arg}(z)>0
, then the exponential
e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
decays and the integral is bounded by a constant. In this case, the pre-factor to the integral in
(II)
is
e^{\frac{\pi}{2}t}
, which cancels the just demonstrated
e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}t}
behavior of the integral. Thus, when
\textup{Arg}(z)>0
,
(II)_{D}\lesssim 1
.
If
\textup{Arg}(z)<0
, then the exponential
e^{\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
grows and the integral is hence bounded by
e^{-\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}
. Combining this with the pre-factor in
(II)
shows that when
\textup{Arg}(z)<0
,
(II)_{D}\lesssim e^{-|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}\lesssim 1
since
t>t_{0}
.

Controlling the integral in region
E
works similarly. Here
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|r|+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
. Since here
r\in(-t-t_{0},-t+t_{0})
, we can bound the integral of
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)
on this region by a constant times
e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}t}e^{-\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t}
. Putting this together with the pre-factors in
(II)
shows that
(II)_{E}\lesssim e^{-|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}\lesssim 1
since
t>2t_{0}
.

In region
F
,
F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|r|+|r+t|)+\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot r}
. Since
r<-t-t_{0}
,
|r|+|r+t|=-2r-t
and since
d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}<0
,
|r+t|^{d+\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim 1
. Thus,

F_{II}(r;d,\sigma,t,z)\lesssim e^{(\pi+\textup{Arg}(z))\cdot r+\frac{\pi}{2}t}.

We can bound the integral of this over the range
r\in(-\infty,-t-t_{0})
by a constant times
e^{-(\pi+\textup{Arg}(z))\cdot t+\frac{\pi}{2}t}=e^{-\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t-\frac{\pi}{2}t}
. Combining this with the pre-factors in
(II)
we find that
(II)_{F}\lesssim e^{-|\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t|}\lesssim 1
since
t>2t_{0}
.
Thus, we have shown that
(II)\lesssim 1
which implies the desired bound (
294
) and hence completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

9.1.5
Jacobi theta function

Jacobi theta functions (see
[
47
, Chapter 10]
) are defined in the
following way for complex
\nu,\rho
with

\textup{Im}(\rho)>0

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(\nu|\rho)=\dfrac{1}{{i}}\!\!\!\sum\limits_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!(-1)^{k}e^{\pi{i}\rho(k+1/2)^{2}}e^{\pi{i}\nu(2k+1)},

\displaystyle\theta_{2}(\nu|\rho)=\!\!\!\sum\limits_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!e^{\pi{i}\rho(k+1/2)^{2}}e^{\pi{i}\nu(2k+1)},

(302)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(\nu|\rho)=\!\!\!\sum\limits_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!e^{\pi{i}\rho k^{2}}e^{2k\pi{i}\nu},

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(\nu|\rho)=\!\!\!\sum\limits_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!(-1)^{k}e^{\pi{i}\rho k^{2}}e^{2k\pi{i}\nu}.

(303)

For every value of
\rho
in this half-plane the functions are entire
functions of
\nu
. Note that
\theta_{1}(\nu|\rho)
is an odd function in
\nu
and
all others are even.
We also need the following identities
[
47
, (78.32), (78.33), (79.7), (79.9) ]
:

\begin{split}\theta_{1}\left(\nu\lvert\rho\right)&=-{i}e^{\pi{i}\rho/4}\cdot e^{\pi{i}\nu}\cdot\left(e^{2\pi{i}\rho},e^{2\pi{i}(\rho+\nu)},e^{-2\pi{i}\nu};e^{2\pi{i}\rho}\right)_{\infty},\\
\theta_{2}\left(\nu\lvert\rho\right)&=e^{\pi{i}\rho/4}\cdot e^{\pi{i}\nu}\cdot\left(e^{2\pi{i}\rho},-e^{2\pi{i}(\rho+\nu)},-e^{-2\pi{i}\nu};e^{2\pi{i}\rho}\right)_{\infty},\\
\theta_{1}\Big(\tfrac{\nu}{\rho}\Big\lvert-\tfrac{1}{\rho}\Big)&=-{i}\sqrt{\tfrac{\rho}{{i}}}\cdot e^{{i}\pi\nu^{2}/\rho}\cdot\theta_{1}(\nu|\rho),\\
\theta_{4}\Big(\tfrac{\nu}{\rho}\Big|-\tfrac{1}{\rho}\Big)&=\sqrt{\tfrac{\rho}{{i}}}\cdot e^{{i}\pi\nu^{2}/\rho}\cdot\theta_{2}(\nu|\rho),\end{split}

with the principal value of the square root.
Using the
\theta_{1}
identity,

\left(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}=\frac{\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\kappa}}\exp\left(\frac{\kappa}{8}-\frac{\kappa z}{2}+\frac{\kappa z^{2}}{2}\right)}{\left(e^{-\kappa};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}\left(e^{-\kappa(1-z)};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}}\cdot\theta_{1}\Big(z\Big|\tfrac{2\pi{i}}{\kappa}\Big).

(304)

Similarly, using the identities involve
\theta_{4}
and
\theta_{2}
we find that

\left(-e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}=\frac{\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\kappa}}\exp\left(\frac{\kappa}{8}-\frac{\kappa z}{2}+\frac{\kappa z^{2}}{2}\right)}{\left(e^{-\kappa};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}\left(-e^{-\kappa(1-z)};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}}\cdot\theta_{4}\Big(z\Big|\tfrac{2\pi{i}}{\kappa}\Big).

(305)

9.1.6
Theta function bounds

Lemma 9.6

For all
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
there exist
C,c,\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
and all
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\textup{Im}(\nu)|\leq\frac{\alpha}{\kappa}

\bigg\lvert\theta_{1}\left(\nu\bigg\lvert\dfrac{2\pi i}{\kappa}\right)\dfrac{e^{\pi^{2}/(2\kappa)}}{2\sin(\pi\nu)}-1\bigg\rvert\,\,,\,\,\bigg\lvert\theta_{4}\left(\nu\bigg\lvert\dfrac{2\pi i}{\kappa}\right)-1\bigg\lvert\leq C\cdot e^{-\frac{c}{\kappa}}.

(306)

Proof.

We can rewrite theta functions in the following way
[
47
, (76.2)]
:

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(\nu|\rho)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{(k+1/2)^{2}\pi{i}\rho}\sin\left((2k+1)\pi\nu\right),

(307)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(\nu|\rho)

\displaystyle=1+2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{k^{2}\pi{i}\rho}\cos\left(2k\pi\nu\right).

(308)

Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind
T_{k}(x)
and the second kind
U_{k}(x)
with
x\in[-1,1]
are defined in the
following way (see
[
36
, Section 1.8.2]
): For
x=\cos(\nu)
,

T_{k}(x):=\cos(k\nu)
and
U_{k}(x):=\frac{\sin((k+1)\nu)}{\sin(\nu)}
.
Their coefficients are explicitly given by

T_{k}(x)=\dfrac{k}{2}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\dfrac{(-1)^{k}}{k-n}{k-n\choose n}(2x)^{k-2n},\qquad U_{k}(x)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}(-1)^{n}{k-n\choose n}(2x)^{k-2n}.

Inserting the Chebyshev polynomials into these expressions we arrive at

\displaystyle\dfrac{\theta_{1}(\nu|\rho)}{2e^{\pi{i}\rho/4}\sin(\pi\nu)}-1

\displaystyle=2\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{\pi{i}\rho(k^{2}+k)}U_{2k}(\cos(\pi\nu)),\text{ and }

(309)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(\nu|\rho)-1

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e^{\pi{i}\rho k^{2}}T_{2k}(\cos(\pi\nu)).

(310)

We now claim that with
x=\cos(\pi\nu)
, for all
\nu\in\mathbb{C}

|U_{k}(x)|,\,|T_{k}(x)|\,\leq\dfrac{(1+\sqrt{5})^{k+1}}{\sqrt{5}}\cdot\max\Big(|\cos(\pi\nu)|,2^{-1}\Big)^{k}.

(311)

To see this, consider the case
|x|\geq 1/2
and
|x|<1/2
separately. When
|x|\geq 1/2

|U_{k}(x)|,\,|T_{k}(x)|\,\leq|2x|^{k}\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}{k-n\choose n}=|2x|^{k}F(k+1),

where
F(k+1)
is the Fibonacci number. When
|x|<1/2

|U_{k}(x)|,\,|T_{k}(x)|\,\leq|2x|^{k-2\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}{k-n\choose n}\leq F(k+1).

Putting these together we see that
|U_{k}(x)|,\,|T_{k}(x)|\,\leq F(k+1)\max(|2x|,1)^{k}
.
Since the Fibonacci number
F(k+1)
equals the nearest integer to

\tfrac{(1+\sqrt{5})^{k+1}}{2^{k+1}\sqrt{5}}
, multiplying this by
2
clearly yields an upper bound on
F(k+1)
and combined with our earlier bounds on
|U_{k}(x)|
and
|T_{k}(x)|
we arrive at (
311
).

Inserting (
311
) into (
309
) we find that

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\dfrac{\theta_{1}(\nu|\rho)}{2e^{\pi{i}\rho/4}\sin(\pi\nu)}-1\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{2(1+\sqrt{5})}{\sqrt{5}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{\pi{i}\rho(k^{2}+k)}(1+\sqrt{5})^{2k}\cdot\max(|\cos(\pi\nu)|,2^{-1})^{2k},

(312)

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert\theta_{4}(\nu|\rho)-1\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{2(1+\sqrt{5})}{\sqrt{5}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{\pi{i}\rho k^{2}}(1+\sqrt{5})^{2k}\cdot\max(|\cos(\pi\nu)|,2^{-1})^{2k}.

(313)

Since
\rho=2\pi{i}/\kappa
it follows that
e^{\pi{i}\rho}=e^{-2\pi^{2}/\kappa}<1
. Thus for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we can bound
e^{\pi{i}\rho(k^{2}+k)},e^{\pi{i}\rho k^{2}}\leq e^{\pi{i}\rho k}
. This shows that for some
C>0
,

\displaystyle\qquad\bigg\lvert\dfrac{\theta_{1}(\nu|\rho)}{2e^{\pi{i}\rho/4}\sin(\pi\nu)}-1\bigg\rvert\,\,,\,\,\bigg\lvert\theta_{4}(\nu|\rho)-1\bigg\rvert\leq

(314)

\displaystyle Ce^{-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{\kappa}}\cdot\max(|\cos(\pi\nu)|,2^{-1})^{2}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{\kappa}ak}(1+\sqrt{5})^{2k}\cdot\max(|\cos(\pi\nu)|,2^{-1})^{2k}.

(315)

Observe that
|\cos(\pi\nu)|\leq e^{|\textup{Im}(\pi\nu)|}
. We have assumed that
|\textup{Im}(\nu)|\leq\frac{\alpha}{\kappa}
with
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
and thus it follows that there exists some
c>0
such that

e^{-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{\kappa}}\cdot\max(|\cos(\pi\nu)|,2^{-1})^{2}\leq e^{-\frac{c}{\kappa}}.

Plugging this bound into (
314
) yields (
306
) and hence the lemma.
∎

9.1.7
Mellin transform

For a function
f(x)
on
(0,+\infty)
, and
s\in\mathbb{C}
, define

F(s)=M[f;s]:=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}f(x)x^{s-1}dx,

(316)

The largest open strip
-\infty\leq a<\textup{Re}(s)\leq b\leq\infty)
in which the integral converges is called the
fundamental
