strip
or the
strip of analyticity
of
M\left[f;s\right]
. Note
that if
g
is defined by the relation
f(x)=g(-\log x),

\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}x^{s-1}g(-\log x)dx=\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-ts}g(t)dt=M\left[f;s\right].

Therefore, all basic properties of the Mellin transform follow from those of the Laplace transform.
The following inversion formula can be found as
[
10
, Theorem 11.2.1.1]
(other similar statements abound).

Proposition 9.7

Assume that the function
F(s)
is analytic in the strip
(a,c)
and satisfies

\lvert F(s)\rvert\leq K\cdot|s|^{-2}

for some
K>0
. Then, for
b\in(a,c)
,

f(x)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{b-{i}\infty}^{b+{i}\infty}F(s)x^{-s}ds

is a continuous function of the variable
x\in(0;\infty)
and does not depend on the choice of
b.
Furthermore,
F(x)=M\left[f;s\right],
and we then say that
f(x)
is the inverse Mellin transform of
F(s)
.

9.2
Proof of Proposition
2.3

The following is the key to being able to use the above derived asymptotics on special function to access
q
-Pochhammer asymptotics.

Lemma 9.8

For all
c>1,

\kappa>0,

\textup{Re}(z)>0
we have that

\displaystyle\log\left(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa}\right)

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{c-{i}\infty}^{c+{i}\infty}\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\dfrac{ds}{\kappa^{s}},

(317)

\displaystyle\log\left(-e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa}\right)

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{c-{i}\infty}^{c+{i}\infty}(2^{-s}-1)\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\dfrac{ds}{\kappa^{s}}

(318)

Proof.

Our goal is to show that
\log\left(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa}\right)
and
\log\left(-e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa}\right)
are the inverse Mellin transforms of the corresponding functions on the right-hand side of (
317
). To do this we compute the Mellin transforms of the left-hand sides in (
317
) and then show they can be inverted.

For
a\in\mathbb{C}
with
|a|<1
and
q\in(0,1)
we may write

-\log(a;q)_{\infty}=-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\log(1-aq^{k})=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{(aq^{k})^{n}}{n}=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{a^{n}}{n}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}q^{nk}=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{a^{n}}{n(1-q^{n})}.

(319)

The interchange of the order of summations is possible due to Fubini’s theorem since for each
q\in(0,1)
there exists
C=C(q)>0
such that

\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigg\lvert\dfrac{a^{n}}{n(1-x^{n})}\bigg\rvert\leq C\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}|a^{n}|<\infty.

Let
f_{1}(x)=-\log\left((e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}\right)
. For
\textup{Re}(z)>0
and
\textup{Re}(s)>1
,

\displaystyle M\left[f_{1};s\right]=-\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\log\left((e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}\right)\kappa^{s-1}d\kappa=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{(e^{-\kappa z})^{n}}{n(1-e^{-n\kappa})}\kappa^{s-1}d\kappa

(320)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{n^{s+1}}\dfrac{e^{-\rho z}\rho^{s-1}}{(1-e^{-\rho})}d\rho=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{n^{s+1}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{e^{-\rho z}\rho^{s-1}}{(1-e^{-\rho})}d\rho=\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z).

(321)

The first equality in (
321
) is by the definition of the Mellin transform. The second equality in (
321
) uses (
319
) with
a=e^{-\kappa z}
and
q=e^{-\kappa}
. The third equality in (
321
) comes from the change of variables
\kappa=\rho/n
. The fourth equality in (
321
) is valid because

\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{n^{\textup{Re}(s)+1}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\bigg\lvert\dfrac{e^{-\rho z}\rho^{s-1}}{(1-e^{-\rho})}d\rho\bigg\rvert\leq C\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{n^{\textup{Re}(s)+1}}<\infty.

The constant term
C>0
above comes from bounding the integral: Since
\textup{Re}(s)>1
, the behavior near
\rho=0
is like
\rho^{\textup{Re}(s)-2}
which is integrable since
\textup{Re}(s)>1
; and near
\rho=\infty
the integrand decays exponentially because
\textup{Re}(z)>0
. The final equality in (
321
) uses (
277
) for
\zeta(s,z)
.

A similar computation for
f_{2}(x)=-\log\left((-e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}\right)
, yields

\displaystyle M[f_{2};s]

\displaystyle=-\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\log\left((-e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}\right)\kappa^{s-1}d\kappa=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^{n}}{n^{s+1}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{e^{-\rho z}\rho^{s-1}}{(1-e^{-\rho})}d\rho

(322)

\displaystyle=-\Gamma(s)\eta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)=(2^{-s}-1)\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z).

(323)

The last equality uses (
282
).

Having computed the Mellin transform for
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
we now verify that they can be inverted using Proposition
9.7
. We must check analyticity and the quadratic decay estimate.
Due to the analyticity of
\Gamma(s),\zeta(s)
and
\zeta(s,z)
, the analyticity of these Mellin transforms holds for
\textup{Re}(s)>1
.

Now we argue that there is quadratic decay. To apply Proposition
9.7
, it suffices to have this decay on any vertical strip. Fix
a=1
and any
c>1
. We claim that there exists a constant
K>0
such that for all
s\in\mathbb{C}
with
\textup{Re}(s)\in(a,c)
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\textup{Re}(z)>0
,

\lvert\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\rvert\leq K|s|^{-2}.

This follows by appealing to the gamma function decay bound in Lemma
9.1
, the boundedness of
\zeta(s+1)
for
\textup{Re}(s)\in(a,c)
, and the bound on
|\zeta(s,z)|
from Lemma
9.3
.
With this, we can invert the Mellin transform of
f_{1}
and thus prove the desired formula. The case for
f_{2}
is similar since the factor
(2^{-s}-1)
is bounded by a constant provided
\textup{Re}(s)\in(a,c)
.
∎

9.2.1
Proof of Proposition
2.3
: asymptotics of

(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}

We will first consider the case when
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
. This is addressed in three steps. Then, in a fourth step we will use the functional identity (
304
) to address the case when
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
. In the fifth and final step, we will combine these two bounds into a common bound.

Step 1.
We start with the representation for
(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}
from Lemma
9.8
and shift the contour of integration to the left of zero, picking up some residues. We claim the following formula: For any non-integer
a<0
,

\displaystyle-\log(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{a-{i}\infty}^{a+{i}\infty}\!\!\!\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}ds

(324)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits^{\ell=1}_{\ell=\lceil a\rceil}\textup{Res}_{s=\ell}\left[\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}\right].

(325)

The starting point for this is (
317
) where the contour has real part
c>1
. The idea is to shift the contour to the left until it lies on a vertical line with real part
a<0
. In doing this, we encounter poles at
s=1,0,-1,\ldots,\lceil a\rceil
whose residues must be accounted for. The summation in (
324
) is precisely the contribution of those residues. To justify the deformation we use Lemmas
9.1
and
9.2
with (
296
) from Proposition
9.5
to show that for
z
fixed with
\textup{Re}(z)>0
there exists some
\varepsilon>0
and
C>0
such that for all
s
with
\textup{Re}(s)=\sigma\in[a,c]
and
|t|>1
,

\left|\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}\right|\leq Ce^{-\varepsilon|t|}.

Step 2.
Next, we compute the residues in (
324
). In order to do that this, we make use of the following Taylor series expansions. We first address the residue at
s=0
. By using the results in (
280
) we see that around
s=0

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{s^{2}}\Gamma(s+1)\cdot\left(s\zeta(s+1)\right)\cdot\zeta(s,z)\cdot\kappa^{-s}=

(326)

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{1}{2}-z)s^{-2}+\left(\left(z-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\log\kappa+\log\left[\tfrac{\Gamma(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]\right)s^{-1}+\cdots,

(327)

where
\cdots
here represents lower order terms in
s
. From this expansion it immediately follows that the residue at
s=0
is
\left(z-\frac{1}{2}\right)\log\kappa+\log\left[\frac{\Gamma(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]
. Turning to the residue at
s=1
, from (
279
) we have that

\underset{s=1}{\textup{Res}}\left[\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}\right]=\dfrac{\Gamma(1)\zeta(2)}{\kappa}\cdot\underset{s=1}{\textup{Res}}\left[\zeta(s,z)\right]=\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{6\kappa}.

The residue at
s=-n
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
is evaluated by (
274
) and (
284
) as

\underset{s=-n}{\textup{Res}}\left[\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}\right]=\underset{s=-n}{\textup{Res}}\left[\Gamma(s)\right]\cdot\zeta(-n+1)\zeta(-n,z)\cdot\kappa^{n}=\dfrac{B_{n}B_{n+1}(z)}{n(n+1)!}\cdot\kappa^{n}.

Recall that Bernoulli numbers are zero for odd integers. Combining these deductions, we conclude for
\textup{Re}(z)>0
and non-integer
a<0
we have

\displaystyle-\log(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{a-{i}\infty}^{a+{i}\infty}\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}ds

(328)

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{6\kappa}+

\displaystyle\left(z-\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\log\kappa+\log\left[\dfrac{\Gamma(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\lfloor-a\rfloor}\dfrac{B_{n+1}(z)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}.

(329)

Recall
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
in the statement of Proposition
2.3
. For any
a\in(-m,-m+1)

we may compare the right-hand sides of (
328
) and (
39
) to see that the error term
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]
in (
39
) is precisely give by

{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]=\dfrac{-1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{a-{i}\infty}^{a+{i}\infty}\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}ds.

(330)

Therefore, our problem reduces to bounding the absolute value of the above integral. Fix some
a\in(-m,-m+1)
. To estimate
|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]|
, we bring the absolute value inside the integral and utilize the bounds given in Propositions
9.2
and
9.5
. Using the notation
s=a+{i}t
, we will divide the integral into small
|t|
and large
|t|
. On account of the just mentioned lemma and propositions, for all
d\in(0,1/2)
such that
a+d\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, there exists a
t_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that for all

t<t_{0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
,

\displaystyle\left|\Gamma(a+{i}t)\zeta(a+{i}t+1)\zeta(a+{i}t,z)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

(331)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\Big(e^{\lvert\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t\rvert}\big(|z|^{1-a}+|t|^{1/2-a}|z|^{-d-a}\big)+|z|^{-d-a}\Big),

(332)

while for all
t>t_{0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
,

\displaystyle\qquad\left|\Gamma(a+{i}t)\zeta(a+{i}t+1)\zeta(a+{i}t,z)\right|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|t|}\times

(333)

\displaystyle\times\Big(e^{\lvert\textup{Arg}(z)\cdot t\rvert}\big(|z|^{-a}+(1+|t|)^{-1}|z|^{1-a}+|t|^{1/2-a}|z|^{-d-a}\big)+|t|^{1/2-a}|z|^{-d-a}\Big),

(334)

In deriving the above we made some simplifications from the bound in Proposition
9.5
.
For
|t|\leq t_{0}
, we bounded
\max(1,|t|^{1/2-a})\leq C

while
for
|t|\geq t_{0}
, we bounded
\max(1,|t|^{1/2-a})\leq C|t|^{1/2-a}

where
C>0
depends on
a
and
t_{0}
.
Since we are presently assuming that
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
(and hence
|z|\geq 1/2
) we find a constant only dependent on
a
and
t_{0}
such that for
|t|\leq t_{0}
,
|z|^{-a}+(1+|t|)^{-1}|z|^{1-a}\leq C|z|^{1-a}
and likewise find
C>0
only dependent on
d+a
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor-d-a\rfloor}|z|^{k}\leq C|z|^{-d-a}
.

With the
t_{0}
above we may bound

|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]|\leq(\pi)^{-1}\kappa^{-a}(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II})

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\left|\Gamma(a+{i}t)\zeta(a+{i}t+1)\zeta(a+{i}t,z)\right|dt,

(335)

\displaystyle\mathbf{II}

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\left|\Gamma(a+{i}t)\zeta(a+{i}t+1)\zeta(a+{i}t,z)\right|dt.

(336)

Estimating
\mathbf{I}
from (
331
) is done easily since
t<t_{0}
and
|z|\geq 1/2
. The main contribution is from the term
|z|^{1-a}
and all other terms can be bounded by it. Thus, we find that there exists
C>0
depending on
a
and
t_{0}
such that

\mathbf{I}\leq C|z|^{1-a}.

To control
\mathbf{II}
requires a bit more. Let us recall two facts. The first is an immediate consequence of the gamma function integral formula: For any
\alpha>-1
and any
{\varepsilon}>0
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-{\varepsilon}t}t^{\alpha}dt={\varepsilon}^{-\alpha-1}\Gamma(\alpha+1).

(337)

The second is that for all
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
, there exists a
C>0
such that

\frac{\pi}{2}-|\textup{Arg}(z)|\geq C|z|^{-1}
.
With these facts we may show that there exists
C>0
depending only on
a
such that

\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}e^{-(\frac{\pi}{2}-\textup{Arg}(z))|t|}\big(|z|^{-a}+(1+|t|)^{-1}|z|^{1-a}+|t|^{1/2-a}|z|^{-d-a}\big)dt\leq C\left(|z|^{1-a}+|z|^{\frac{3}{2}-2a-d}\right).

(338)

To derive this inequality we first extended the integration to
(0,\infty)
, and then used (
337
) with
{\varepsilon}=\frac{\pi}{2}-\textup{Arg}(z)\geq C|z|^{-1}
. Similarly, we find that

\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|t|}|t|^{1/2-a}|z|^{-d-a}dt\leq C|z|^{-d-a}.

(339)

Thus, in light of (
333
) and the above bounds, we have shown that

\mathbf{II}\leq C\left(|z|^{1-a}+|z|^{\frac{3}{2}-2a-d}+|z|^{-d-a}\right),

and combining this with the earlier bound on
\mathbf{I}
we see that there exists
C>0
dependent only on
a
and
d
such that

|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]|\leq C\kappa^{-a}\left(|z|^{1-a}+|z|^{\frac{3}{2}-2a-d}+|z|^{-d-a}\right)\leq C\kappa^{-a}\left(|z|^{1-a}+|z|^{\frac{3}{2}-2a-d}\right).

Since we were allowed to take
a\in(-m,-m+1)
arbitrary, and
d\in(0,1/2)
provided
a+d\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, we may try to optimize the right-hand side above. Let
b=-a\in(m-1,m)
. Then, we have shown that for any
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/2)
and any
b\in(m-1,m)
, there exists
C>0
such that

|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]|\leq C\kappa^{b}\left(|z|^{1+b}+|z|^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}\right)\leq C\kappa^{b}|z|^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}.

(340)

This completes the proof of the proposition when
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
. Notice that there is no restriction on
\textup{Im}(z)
assumed here (in accordance with the statement of the proposition).

Step 4.
We will now make use of the functional identity (
304
) to relate the
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
behavior to the
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
result we have proven already above.
The two Pochhammer symbols,
(e^{-\kappa};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}
and
(e^{-\kappa(1-z)};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}
, which arise on the right-hand side of (
304
) are both of the form
(e^{-\kappa\tilde{z}};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}
for
\tilde{z}=1
and
1-z
respectively. In both cases (in the second case, due to the assumption that
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
), we have that
\textup{Re}(\tilde{z})\geq 1/2
, hence the result we have proved above can be applied. In particular, for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, recalling
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}[\kappa,z]
from (
37
),

\displaystyle-\log(e^{-\kappa(1-z)};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

(341)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{A}}^{+}[\kappa,1-z]

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}\dfrac{B_{n+1}(1-z)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}+{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}[\kappa,1-z].

(342)

\displaystyle-\log\left(e^{-\kappa};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}=

(343)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{A}}^{+}[\kappa,1]

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}\dfrac{B_{n+1}(1)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}+{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}[\kappa,1].

(344)

Recall that from (
283
) we have
B_{n+1}(1-z)=(-1)^{n+1}B_{n+1}(z)
and that, in particular,
B_{n+1}=B_{n+1}(1)=0
for
n
even. Thus, we may replace
B_{n+1}(1-z)B_{n}
by
-B_{n+1}(z)B_{n}
(when
n
is even, both sides are zero). Also observe that the product
B_{n+1}B_{n}=0
for all
n
except
n=1
.
By the Euler reflection equation

\log\left[\frac{\Gamma(1-z)}{\sqrt{2}\pi}\right]=-\log\left[2\sin(\pi z)\right]-\log\left[\frac{\Gamma(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\right]
.
Plugging this into (
304
) we find that we can write

\log\left(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}={\mathcal{A}}^{+}[\kappa,z]-\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}\dfrac{B_{n+1}(z)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}+{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]

where

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}\dfrac{B_{n+1}(1)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}+\dfrac{\kappa}{8}-\dfrac{\kappa z}{2}+\dfrac{\kappa z^{2}}{2}+{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}(\kappa,1)+{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}(\kappa,1-z)

(345)

\displaystyle\qquad+\log\left[\theta_{1}\left(z\bigg\lvert\dfrac{2\pi{i}}{\kappa}\right)\dfrac{e^{\pi^{2}/(2\kappa)}}{2\sin(\pi z)}\right].

(346)

This provides an explicit formula for the error term
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]
in (
39
).
The first bound in Lemma
9.6
shows that for any
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
there exist
C,c,\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa}

\left|\log\left(\theta_{1}\left(z\bigg\lvert\dfrac{2\pi{i}}{\kappa}\right)\dfrac{e^{\pi^{2}/(2\kappa)}}{2\sin(\pi z)}\right)\right|\leq\log\big(1+C\cdot e^{-\frac{c}{\kappa}}\big)\leq C\cdot e^{-\frac{c}{\kappa}}.

The two terms
{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}(\kappa,1)
and
{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}(\kappa,1-z)
were bounded in the first three steps of this proof, see (
340
). The summation involving Bernoulli numbers has only one non-zero term when
n=1
and is proportional to
\kappa
. Thus, to summarize so far we have shown that for any
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
,
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/2)
and
b\in(m-1,m)
there exist
C,c,\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa}

|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]|\leq C\left(\kappa+\kappa|1-2z|^{2}+\kappa^{b}|1-z|^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}+\kappa^{b}+e^{-\frac{c}{\kappa}}\right).

(347)

For each
b
, provided
\kappa
is small enough, we can bound
e^{-\frac{c}{\kappa}}\leq\kappa^{b}
. Since
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
it follows that there exist a constant
C
which depends on
b
such that
1\leq C|1-z|^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}
. Thus, we can simplify our bound to the following: For any
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
,
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/2)
and
b\in(m-1,m)
there exist
C,c,\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa}

|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]|\leq C\left(\kappa+\kappa|1-2z|^{2}+\kappa^{b}|1-z|^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}\right).

(348)

Step 5.
In this final step, we combine the bound (
340
) shown in Step 3 for all
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
with the bound just shown at the end of Step 4, in (
348
) which holds for
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
(with some additional condition on the imaginary part). Notice that if
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
, then there exists a constant
C
such that
|z|\leq C(1+|z|)
, and similarly, if
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
, then there exists a constant
C
such that
|1-2z|\leq C(1+|z|)
. Making these replacements in the respective bounds (
340
) and (
348
) immediately leads to the error bound claimed in (
41
) for
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]
. This completes the proof of the asymptotics of
\log(q^{z};q)_{\infty}
provided
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa}
. We will extend this asymptotic to
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{2\alpha}{\kappa}
using the result for
\log(-q^{z};q)_{\infty}
. To avoid confusion we defer this until the end of the proof of the
\log(-q^{z};q)_{\infty}
asymptotics.

9.2.2
Proof of Proposition
2.3
: asymptotics of
(-q^{z};q)_{\infty}

Comparing the formula (
317
) for
(q^{z};q)_{\infty}
to the formula (
318
) for
(-q^{z};q)_{\infty}
, the only difference is the inclusion of the factor
2^{-s}-1
in the later. This will have very minor effect on the argument, as compared to our earlier study of the asymptotics of
(q^{z};q)_{\infty}
in Section
9.2.1
. As such, we just summarize the outcome of each of the five steps from that proof, subject to inclusion now of the multiplicative factor
2^{-s}-1
. As a sixth and final step we include the extension of the
\log(q^{z};q)_{\infty}
asymptotic to only assume
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{2\alpha}{\kappa}
.

Step 1.
Assume that
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
. Under this condition we may use (
318
) and decay estimates to show, as in (
324
), that for non-integer
a<0
,

\displaystyle-\log(-e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{a-{i}\infty}^{a+{i}\infty}\!\!\!(2^{-s}-1)\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}ds

(349)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum\limits^{\ell=1}_{\ell=\lceil a\rceil}\!\!\textup{Res}_{s=\ell}\left[(2^{-s}-1)\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}\right].

(350)

Step 2.
We compute the residues in (
349
). Notice that around
s=0
, we have
2^{-s}-1=-s\log(2)+\cdots
. From this we see that around
s=0
,

(2^{-s}-1)\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}=\frac{(z-\frac{1}{2})\log 2}{s}+\cdots

where the
\cdots
represent lower order terms. Hence the residue at
s=0
is
(z-\tfrac{1}{2})\log 2
. At
s=1
, the factor
2^{-s}-1
is simply evaluated to
-\tfrac{1}{2}
and hence the residue there becomes
-\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{12\kappa}
. Finally, for the residues at
-n
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the
2^{-s}-1
factor contributes a new multiplicative factor
2^{n}-1
to the residue. Thus, (
328
) is replaced by (recall
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}[\kappa,z]
from (
38
))

\displaystyle-\log(e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{a-{i}\infty}^{a+{i}\infty}(2^{-s}-1)\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}ds

(351)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{A}}^{-}[\kappa,z]+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\lfloor-a\rfloor}(2^{n}-1)\dfrac{B_{n+1}(z)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}.

(352)

Step 3.
We fix
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and let
a\in(-m,-m+1)
. Comparing the right-hand sides of (
351
) and (
40
) we see that the error term
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]
in (
40
) is precisely give by

{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]=\dfrac{-1}{2\pi{i}}\int\limits_{a-{i}\infty}^{a+{i}\infty}(2^{-s}-1)\Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\zeta(s,z)\kappa^{-s}ds.

(353)

Bounding the absolute value of the error term
|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]|
proceeds quite similar to that of
|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]|
in (
330
). Since along our integration contour,
\textup{Re}(s)=a
, it follows that we can bound
|2^{-s}-1|\leq 2^{-a}+1
which can be absorbed into the constant
C
. Thus, we arrive as the same bound as in (
340
). In particular, we see that for any
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/2)
and
b\in(m-1,m)
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]|\leq C\kappa^{b}|z|^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}.

(354)

This result still assumes that
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
.

Step 4.
We now return to
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
and use (
305
) to deduce the asymptotics in that regime from those derived above for
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
.
Since
\textup{Re}(1-z)\geq 1/2
, we may conclude from the results of Steps 1–3 above that for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

-\log(-e^{-\kappa(1-z)};e^{-\kappa})_{\infty}\!\!\!=-{\mathcal{A}}^{-}[\kappa,1-z]+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}\left(2^{n}-1\right)\dfrac{B_{n+1}(1-z)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}+{\mathsf{Err}}^{-}_{m}[\kappa,1-z].

(355)

Combining this with the asymptotics of
-\log\left(e^{-\kappa};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}

in (
341
) and the form of
the right-hand sides of (
40
) in
(
40
) yields

\displaystyle\log\left(-e^{-\kappa z};e^{-\kappa}\right)_{\infty}={\mathcal{A}}^{-}[\kappa,z]-\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}(2^{n}-1)\dfrac{B_{n+1}(z)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}+{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]

(356)

where
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}\dfrac{B_{n+1}(1)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}+\dfrac{\kappa}{8}-\dfrac{\kappa z}{2}+\dfrac{\kappa z^{2}}{2}+{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{\lfloor-d\rfloor}[\kappa,1]+{\mathsf{Err}}^{-}_{\lfloor-d\rfloor}[\kappa,1-z]

(357)

\displaystyle\quad+\log\Big(\theta_{4}\Big(z\Big|\tfrac{2\pi{i}}{\kappa}\Big)\Big).

(358)

We may now invoke the second bound in Lemma
9.6
which shows that for any
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
there exists
C,c,\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa}

\left|\log\left(\theta_{4}\left(z\bigg\lvert\frac{2\pi{i}}{\kappa}\right)\right)\right|\leq\log\big(1+C\cdot e^{-\frac{c}{\kappa}}\big)\leq C\cdot e^{-\frac{c}{\kappa}}.

The term
{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}(\kappa,1)
was bounded in Section
9.2.1
whereas
{\mathsf{Err}}^{-}_{m}(\kappa,1-z)
has been already bounded in the first three steps of this proof, see (
354
). Thus, in the same manner as in Section
9.2.1
we conclude that: For any
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
,
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/2)
and
b\in(m-1,m)
there exist
C,c,\kappa_{0}>0
such that for all
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
and all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa}
(again, this is assuming
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
)

|{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]|\leq C\left(\kappa+\kappa|1-2z|^{2}+\kappa^{b}|1-z|^{1+2b+{\varepsilon}}\right).

Step 5.
The synthesis of the
\textup{Re}(z)\geq 1/2
and
\textup{Re}(z)<1/2
bounds on the error are precisely the same as above, hence the proof is complete.

Step 6.
The fact that the bound on
{\mathsf{Err}}^{+}_{m}[\kappa,z]
holds with
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{2\alpha}{\kappa}
follows from (
41
) for
{\mathsf{Err}}^{\pm}_{m}[\kappa,z]
with
|\textup{Im}(z)|<\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa}
by using the transformation (
42
). To see this, consider
z
with
\textup{Im}(z)\in[\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa},\tfrac{2\alpha}{\kappa})
(the negative imaginary case is the same). Then

\displaystyle\log(q^{z};q)_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\log(-q^{z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i}};q)_{\infty}

(359)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{A}}^{-}[\kappa,z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i}]-\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}\left(2^{n}-1\right)\dfrac{B_{n+1}(z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i})B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}+{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i}].

(360)

Comparing this to (
39
) shows that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]

\displaystyle={\mathcal{A}}^{-}[\kappa,z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i}]-{\mathcal{A}}^{+}[\kappa,z]

(361)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{n=1}^{m-1}\left(\dfrac{B_{n+1}(z)B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}-\left(2^{n}-1\right)\dfrac{B_{n+1}(z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i})B_{n}}{n(n+1)!}\kappa^{n}\right)+{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i}].

(362)

For

\textup{Im}(z)\in[\tfrac{\alpha}{\kappa},\tfrac{2\alpha}{\kappa})
we
have that
\textup{Im}[z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i}]\in[\frac{\alpha-\pi}{\kappa},\frac{2\alpha-\pi}{\kappa})
which is
contained in
(-\frac{\beta}{\kappa},\frac{\beta}{\kappa})
for some

\beta\in(0,\pi)
. Thus we can apply the bound (
41
)
to control
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i}]
. All of the
other terms are also easily controlled (for the Gamma function, use
the asymptotics from Lemma
9.1
) and doing so we
verify that (
41
) holds for
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{+}[\kappa,z]
. The reason why we cannot do the same extension of the range of
imaginary part for
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{-}[\kappa,z]
is that in replicating that
above argument we encounter
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}[\kappa,z-\frac{\pi}{\kappa}{i}]-{\mathcal{A}}^{-}[\kappa,z]
and the first term may have singularities from
the Gamma function.

Note that the above argument works for obtaining a bound on
{\mathsf{Err}}_{m}^{\pm}[\kappa,z]
for
z
with
\textup{dist}(\textup{Re}(z),\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0})>r
for any
r>0
since we will avoid the singularities from the Gamma function.
This implies the final claim of the proposition.

). We would also like to thank Wlodek Bryc for asking clarifying questions about Proposition
